The flightiness of the female temperament is very evident in those who have not arrived at the years which teach how to hide faults and frailties, and, therefore, indiscretions bristle from a young girl the way branches do from a bush.”

“The person who would deny that﻿—” said Meehawl.

“Female children, however, have the particular sanction of nature. They are produced in astonishing excess over males, and may, accordingly, be admitted as dominant to the male; but the well-proven law that the minority shall always control the majority will relieve our minds from a fear which might otherwise become intolerable.”

“It’s true enough,” said Meehawl. “Have you noticed, sir, that in a litter of pups﻿—”

“I have not,” said the Philosopher. “Certain trades and professions, it is curious to note, tend to be perpetuated in the female line. The sovereign profession among bees and ants is always female, and publicans also descend on the distaff side. You will have noticed that every publican has three daughters of extraordinary charms. Lacking these signs we would do well to look askance at such a man’s liquor, divining that in his brew there will be an undue percentage of water, for if his primogeniture is infected how shall his honesty escape?”

“It would take a wise head to answer that,” said Meehawl.

“It would not,” said the Philosopher. “Throughout nature the female tends to polygamy.”

“If,” said Meehawl, “that unfortunate daughter of mine is lying dead in a ditch﻿—”

“It doesn’t matter,” said the Philosopher. “Many races have endeavoured to place some limits to this increase in females. Certain Oriental peoples have conferred the titles of divinity on crocodiles, serpents, and tigers of the jungle, and have fed these with their surplusage of daughters. In China, likewise, such sacrifices are defended as honourable and economic practices. But, broadly speaking, if daughters have to be curtailed I prefer your method of losing them rather than the religio-hysterical compromises of the Orient.”

“I give you my word, sir,” said Meehawl, “that I don’t know what you are talking about at all.”

===Chinese===
哲学家把椅子朝米霍尔拉近了些。

「女儿这东西，」他说，「自打设立以来，就是父母操心的一大根源。女子心性的轻浮，在还没活到学会遮掩毛病和弱点的年纪的人身上，表现得再明白不过；所以冒失事儿从年轻姑娘身上支棱出来，就像枝条从灌木上支棱出来一样。」

「谁要驳这一句——」米霍尔说。

「不过女孩儿家自有大自然的特准。她们出生的数量大大超过男孩，多得惊人，照此说来，不妨承认她们对男性占着优势；但少数永远统治多数，这条久经证明的法则，可以宽宽我们的心，不然那恐惧可要叫人受不了。」

「这话不假。」米霍尔说。「先生您留意过没有，一窝小狗崽——」

「没留意过。」哲学家说。「说来奇怪，某些行当和职业，总爱沿母系传下去。蜜蜂和蚂蚁中间，为王这门职业永远是女性的；酒馆老板也是母传女。你一定留意过，每个酒馆老板都有三个魅力非凡的女儿。缺了这些迹象，我们对这种人卖的酒就该斜着眼瞧，料定他的酒里掺水超了份——因为他的长女既已坏掉，他的诚实还怎么保全？」

「这得聪明脑袋才答得上。」米霍尔说。

「用不着。」哲学家说。「放眼自然界，雌性趋于多配。」

「要是我那苦命的丫头真挺在沟里——」米霍尔说。

「不要紧。」哲学家说。「许多种族都曾想法子给这种女性的增殖设限。某些东方民族把神的名号加给鳄鱼、毒蛇和林中猛虎，再拿多余的女儿去喂它们。在中国，这类牺牲同样被辩护为体面而合算的做法。但大体而论，如果女儿非裁减不可，比起东方那种宗教歇斯底里的折衷，我更赞成你这种『丢失』的法子。」

「我向您起誓，先生，」米霍尔说，「您说的这些我压根儿一点不懂。」

===Original===
“That,” said the Philosopher, “may be accounted for in three ways﻿—firstly, there is a lack of cerebral continuity: that is, faulty attention; secondly, it might be due to a local peculiarity in the conformation of the skull, or, perhaps, a superficial instead of a deep indenting of the cerebral coil; and thirdly﻿—”

“Did you ever hear,” said Meehawl, “of the man that had the scalp of his head blown off by a gun, and they soldered the bottom of a tin dish to the top of his skull the way you could hear his brains ticking inside of it for all the world like a Waterbury watch?”

“I did not,” said the Philosopher. “Thirdly, it may﻿—”

“It’s my daughter, Caitilin, sir,” said Meehawl humbly. “Maybe she is lying in the butt of a ditch and the crows picking her eyes out.”

“What did she die of?” said the Philosopher.

“My wife only put it that maybe she was dead, and that maybe she was taken by the fairies, and that maybe she went away with the travelling man that had the musical instrument. She said it was a concertina, but I think myself it was a flute he had.”

“Who was this traveller?”

“I never saw him,” said Meehawl, “but one day I went a few perches up the hill and I heard him playing﻿—thin, squeaky music it was like you’d be blowing out of a tin whistle. I looked about for him everywhere, but not a bit of him could I see.”

“Eh?” said the Philosopher.

“I looked about﻿—” said Meehawl.

“I know,” said the Philosopher. “Did you happen to look at your goats?”

===Chinese===
「这个嘛，」哲学家说，「可以有三层解释——第一，脑子的连贯性欠佳：也就是注意力有毛病；第二，可能是头颅构造上有一处局部特点，或者，脑回压得浅了，没压到深处；第三——」

「先生您听说过那个人没有，」米霍尔说，「头皮叫枪崩掉了，他们把白铁盘子的底焊在他脑壳顶上，隔着盘子都听得见他脑子在里头嘀嗒嘀嗒地走，活脱脱一只沃特伯里怀表（Waterbury watch）？」

「没听说过。」哲学家说。「第三，可能——」

「是我女儿的事啊，卡蒂琳，先生。」米霍尔可怜巴巴地说。「兴许她正躺在哪条沟底，乌鸦在啄她的眼睛。」

「她死于什么病？」哲学家问。

「我老婆不过是说，兴许她死了；又说，兴许叫仙灵带走了；还说，兴许跟那个带乐器的走乡人跑了。她说那是架六角手风琴（concertina），可依我自己想，他拿的是支笛子。」

「那走乡的是什么人？」

「我从没见过他，」米霍尔说，「不过有一天我往山上走了几竿远，听见他在吹——那音乐又细又尖，像是从锡哨里吹出来的。我到处找他，可连他一根毫毛也没瞧见。」

「嗯？」哲学家说。

「我到处找——」米霍尔说。

「我知道。」哲学家说。「你可曾碰巧看看你的山羊？」

===Original===
“I couldn’t well help doing that,” said Meehawl.

“What were they doing?” said the Philosopher eagerly.

“They were bucking each other across the field, and standing on their hind legs and cutting such capers that I laughed till I had a pain in my stomach at the gait of them.”

“This is very interesting,” said the Philosopher.

“Do you tell me so?” said Meehawl.

“I do,” said the Philosopher, “and for this reason﻿—most of the races of the world have at one time or another﻿—”

“It’s my little daughter, Caitilin, sir,” said Meehawl.

“I’m attending to her,” the Philosopher replied.

“I thank you kindly,” returned Meehawl.

The Philosopher continued﻿—

“Most of the races of the world have at one time or another been visited by this deity, whose title is the ‘Great God Pan,’ but there is no record of his ever having journeyed to Ireland, and, certainly within historic times, he has not set foot on these shores. He lived for a great number of years in Egypt, Persia, and Greece, and although his empire is supposed to be worldwide, this universal sway has always been, and always will be, contested; but nevertheless, however sharply his empire may be curtailed, he will never be without a kingdom wherein his exercise of sovereign rights will be gladly and passionately acclaimed.”

“Is he one of the old gods, sir?” said Meehawl.

“He is,” replied the Philosopher, “and his coming intends no good to this country. Have you any idea why he should have captured your daughter?”

“Not an idea in the world.”

===Chinese===
「这个不看也难。」米霍尔说。

「它们在干什么？」哲学家急切地问。

「它们在田里你撞我我撞你，又用后腿直立起来，蹦跶得那叫一个欢，瞧着它们那副走相，我笑得肚子都疼了。」

「这可非常有意思。」哲学家说。

「您这话当真？」米霍尔说。

「当真。」哲学家说，「缘故是这样的——世上大多数种族，早晚都曾——」

「是我家小女儿卡蒂琳的事啊，先生。」米霍尔说。

「我正料理着呢。」哲学家答道。

「多谢您费心。」米霍尔回道。

哲学家接着说——

「世上大多数种族，早晚都曾蒙这位神祇光临，他的名号是『伟大的潘神』（Great God Pan）；可没有任何记载说他到过爱尔兰，而且至少在有史可查的年代里，他从没在这片海岸上落过脚。他在埃及、波斯和希腊住过许多许多年；他的帝国虽说号称遍及寰宇，这份普世的统治权却从来有人争夺，往后也永远有人争夺；不过，无论他的疆土被削得多么小，他永远不缺一个王国——在那里，他行使王权，子民欢欣鼓舞、如痴如狂地拥戴他。」

「他是老一辈的神仙吗，先生？」米霍尔问。

「正是。」哲学家答道，「他到这地方来，可没安着好心。他为什么偏偏掳走你女儿，你可有半点头绪？」

「半点也没有。」

===Original===
“Is your daughter beautiful?”

“I couldn’t tell you, because I never thought of looking at her that way. But she is a good milker, and as strong as a man. She can lift a bag of meal under her arm easier than I can; but she’s a timid creature for all that.”

“Whatever the reason is I am certain that he has the girl, and I am inclined to think that he was directed to her by the Leprecauns of the Gort. You know they are at feud with you ever since their bird was killed?”

“I am not likely to forget it, and they racking me day and night with torments.”

“You may be sure,” said the Philosopher, “that if he’s anywhere at all it’s at Gort na Cloca Mora he is, for, being a stranger, he wouldn’t know where to go unless he was directed, and they know every hole and corner of this countryside since ancient times. I’d go up myself and have a talk with him, but it wouldn’t be a bit of good, and it wouldn’t be any use your going either. He has power over all grown people so that they either go and get drunk or else they fall in love with every person they meet, and commit assaults and things I wouldn’t like to be telling you about. The only folk who can go near him at all are little children, because he has no power over them until they grow to the sensual age, and then he exercises lordship over them as over everyone else. I’ll send my two children with a message to him to say that he isn’t doing the decent thing, and that if he doesn’t let the girl alone and go back to his own country we’ll send for Angus Óg.”

“He’d make short work of him, I’m thinking.”

“He might surely; but he may take the girl for himself all the same.”

“Well, I’d sooner he had her than the other one, for he’s one of ourselves anyhow, and the devil you know is better than the devil you don’t know.”

“Angus Óg is a god,” said the Philosopher severely.

“I know that, sir,” replied Meehawl; “it’s only a way of talking I have. But how will your honour get at Angus? for I heard say that he hadn’t been seen for a hundred years, except one night only when he talked to a man for half an hour on Kilmasheogue.”

“I’ll find him, sure enough,” replied the Philosopher.

===Chinese===
「你女儿生得美吗？」

「这我说不上，我从来没往那上头看过她。不过她挤奶是把好手，力气赛得过男人。一袋面粉她夹在胳膊底下就走，比我还轻巧；话虽如此，她是个胆小的生灵。」

「不管缘故是什么，人准在他手里；我还有一想：指他找到这姑娘的，多半是戈特的矮妖。自从他们的鸟被弄死，他们跟你结了仇，这你知道吧？」

「忘不了的，他们还日夜拿苦刑拧我呢。」

「你可以放心，」哲学家说，「他只要还在什么地方，那地方准是戈特纳克洛卡莫拉；他人生地不熟，没人指点就没处可去，而矮妖们从太古以来就认得这一乡的每一个窟窿每一个角落。我倒想亲自上去跟他谈谈，可半点用也不会有；你去，也一样白搭。他对一切成年人都有法力，叫他中招的人，不是跑去灌得烂醉，就是见一个爱一个，还要动手动脚，干出些我不便对你细说的事来。唯有小孩子能靠近他，因为孩子不到怀春的年纪，他使不上法力；一到年纪，他照旧对他们行使君权。我打算叫我家两个孩子给他捎句话，就说他这事办得不体面，要是他不放过那姑娘、不回他自己的国家去，我们就去请安格斯·奥格（Angus Óg）。」

「我想，他三下五除二就能把他收拾了。」

「多半能；可他也可能把姑娘据为己有，这同是一样。」

「唉，那也比落在那一位手里强，他到底是自家的神，相识的魔鬼总强过不相识的魔鬼。」

「安格斯·奥格是一位神。」哲学家正色道。

「这我知道，先生，」米霍尔回道，「不过是我嘴上的一个说法。可老爷您怎么寻得着安格斯呢？我听说他一百年没露过面了，只有一夜里，他在基尔马肖格（Kilmasheogue）山上跟一个人说了半个钟头的话。」

「我自然寻得着他。」哲学家答道。

===Original===
“I’ll warrant you will,” replied Meehawl heartily as he stood up. “Long life and good health to your honour,” said he as he turned away.

The Philosopher lit his pipe.

“We live as long as we are let,” said he, “and we get the health we deserve. Your salutation embodies a reflection on death which is not philosophic. We must acquiesce in all logical progressions. The merging of opposites is completion. Life runs to death as to its goal, and we should go towards that next stage of experience either carelessly as to what must be, or with a good, honest curiosity as to what may be.”

“There’s not much fun in being dead, sir,” said Meehawl.

“How do you know?” said the Philosopher.

“I know well enough,” replied Meehawl.

===Chinese===
「这话我信得过。」米霍尔起身，诚诚恳恳地答道。「愿老爷长寿康健。」他转身走时说。

哲学家点着了烟斗。

「人家容我们活多久，我们就活多久；我们得到的是我们配得到的健康。」他说。「你这句祝辞里含着对死亡的看法，那看法不哲学。一切合乎逻辑的递进，我们都得默许。对立面的融合即是圆满。生命奔向死亡，如奔向归宿；走向下一程阅历的时候，我们对必然之事，要么满不在乎，要么怀着一腔端正诚实的好奇，去看看那未定之事。」

「死掉了没什么乐子，先生。」米霍尔说。

「你怎么知道？」哲学家说。

「我知道得清楚着呢。」米霍尔回答。

### VIII / 第八章

===Original===
When the children leaped into the hole at the foot of the tree they found themselves sliding down a dark, narrow slant which dropped them softly enough into a little room. This room was hollowed out immediately under the tree, and great care had been taken not to disturb any of the roots which ran here and there through the chamber in the strangest crisscross, twisted fashion. To get across such a place one had to walk round, and jump over, and duck under perpetually. Some of the roots had formed themselves very conveniently into low seats and narrow, uneven tables, and at the bottom all the roots ran into the floor and away again in the direction required by their business. After the clear air outside this place was very dark to the children’s eyes, so that they could not see anything for a few minutes, but after a little time their eyes became accustomed to the semiobscurity and they were able to see quite well. The first things they became aware of were six small men who were seated on low roots. They were all dressed in tight green clothes and little leathern aprons, and they wore tall green hats which wobbled when they moved. They were all busily engaged making shoes. One was drawing out wax ends on his knee, another was softening pieces of leather in a bucket of water, another was polishing the instep of a shoe with a piece of curved bone, another was paring down a heel with a short broad-bladed knife, and another was hammering wooden pegs into a sole. He had all the pegs in his mouth, which gave him a wide-faced, jolly expression, and according as a peg was wanted he blew it into his hand and hit it twice with his hammer, and then he blew another peg, and he always blew the peg with the right end uppermost, and never had to hit it more than twice. He was a person well worth watching.

===Chinese===
孩子们跳进树脚下的洞，只觉得自己顺着一道又黑又窄的斜坡往下滑，软软和和地落进一间小屋。这屋子就掏空在大树正下方，当初挖的时候费了大心，一根树根也没惊动；那些根在屋里四面八方地穿来穿去，绞成最古怪的一团乱麻。要在这样的地方走动，人得不停地绕着走、跳过去、低头钻过去。有几条根长得很顺手，天然成了矮座和窄窄歪歪的小桌；到了底上，所有的根都扎进地面，又各奔各的正务去了。比起外头清爽的天光，这地方在孩子们眼里黑得很，有好几分钟什么也瞧不见；过了一阵，眼睛惯了这半明半暗，便看得相当清楚了。他们头一样认出来的，是坐在低树根上的六个小个子男人。他们都穿着紧身的绿衣裳、系着小小的皮围裙，头上一律高高的绿帽子，一动帽子就晃。六个人都忙着做鞋。一个在膝上搓蜡线，一个在水桶里浸软皮子，一个用一块弯骨打磨鞋面，一个用短阔的刀削鞋跟，还有一个正把木钉敲进鞋底。他把木钉全衔在嘴里，衬得脸宽宽的、乐呵呵的；要用钉子了，他就把一颗吹到手心里，用锤子敲两下，然后又吹一颗；他吹出来的钉子回回都是头朝上，从不用敲第三下。这个人很值得瞧上半天。

===Original===
The children had slid down so unexpectedly that they almost forgot their good manners, but as soon as Seumas Beg discovered that he was really in a room he removed his cap and stood up.

“God be with all here,” said he.

The Leprecaun who had brought them lifted Brigid from the floor to which amazement still constrained her.

“Sit down on that little root, child of my heart,” said he, “and you can knit stockings for us.”

“Yes, sir,” said Brigid meekly.

The Leprecaun took four knitting needles and a ball of green wool from the top of a high, horizontal root. He had to climb over one, go round three and climb up two roots to get at it, and he did this so easily that it did not seem a bit of trouble. He gave the needles and wool to Brigid Beg.

“Do you know how to turn the heel, Brigid Beg?” said he.

“No, sir,” said Brigid.

“Well, I’ll show you how when you come to it.”

===Chinese===
孩子们滑下来得太突然，差点儿把礼貌忘了；可谢默斯·贝格一发觉自己当真是在一间屋子里，立刻摘下帽子，站起身来。

「愿上帝保佑这一家子。」他说。

带他们来的那个矮妖把布里吉德从地上扶起来——她还惊得钉在那儿呢。

「坐到那截小树根上吧，我心肝儿，」他说，「你可以给我们织袜子。」

「好的，先生。」布里吉德乖乖地应道。

矮妖从一条横着的高树根顶上取来四根织针和一团绿毛线。要够着那地方，他得翻过一条根、绕过三条根、再爬上两条根；他做得那样轻巧，仿佛一点不费事。他把针和毛线交给布里吉德·贝格。

「袜跟怎么收，你会吗，布里吉德·贝格？」他问。

「不会，先生。」布里吉德说。

「不碍事，织到那儿我教你。」

===Original===
The other six Leprecauns had ceased work and were looking at the children. Seumas turned to them.

“God bless the work,” said he politely.

One of the Leprecauns, who had a grey, puckered face and a thin fringe of grey whisker very far under his chin, then spoke.

“Come over here, Seumas Beg,” said he, “and I’ll measure you for a pair of shoes. Put your foot up on that root.”

The boy did so, and the Leprecaun took the measure of his foot with a wooden rule.

“Now, Brigid Beg, show me your foot,” and he measured her also. “They’ll be ready for you in the morning.”

“Do you never do anything else but make shoes, sir?” said Seumas.

“We do not,” replied the Leprecaun, “except when we want new clothes, and then we have to make them, but we grudge every minute spent making anything else except shoes, because that is the proper work for a Leprecaun. In the nighttime we go about the country into people’s houses and we clip little pieces off their money, and so, bit by bit, we get a crock of gold together, because, do you see, a Leprecaun has to have a crock of gold so that if he’s captured by men folk he may be able to ransom himself. But that seldom happens, because it’s a great disgrace altogether to be captured by a man, and we’ve practiced so long dodging among the roots here that we can easily get away from them. Of course, now and again we are caught; but men are fools, and we always escape without having to pay the ransom at all. We wear green clothes because it’s the colour of the grass and the leaves, and when we sit down under a bush or lie in the grass they just walk by without noticing us.”

===Chinese===
另外六个矮妖都停了活计，望着两个孩子。谢默斯朝他们转过身。

「愿上帝赐福这活计。」他客客气气地说。

一个矮妖开了口，他脸是灰色的、皱巴巴，下巴底下老远才有一圈稀稀的灰胡子茬。

「过来这边，谢默斯·贝格，」他说，「我给你量一量，做双鞋。把脚搁到那截树根上。」

男孩照办了，矮妖用一根木尺量了他的脚。

「好了，布里吉德·贝格，把你的脚给我瞧瞧。」他又量了她。「明儿早上鞋就做好了。」

「先生，你们除了做鞋，就不干点别的吗？」谢默斯问。

「不干，」矮妖答道，「除非要添新衣裳了，那才不得不自己做；可做鞋以外的活计，每花一分钟我们都心疼，因为做鞋才是矮妖的正经营生。夜里我们到乡间各处钻进人家的屋子，从他们的钱上剪下小小一角；这样一点一点，就攒起一罐金子。因为，你瞧，矮妖非得有一罐金子不可，万一叫人捉住了，好拿它赎身。可这事难得发生，因为叫人捉住是天大的丢脸事；再说我们在这树根中间闪转腾挪，练了这么久，脱身容易得很。当然喽，偶尔也有被抓住的时候；可人是笨蛋，我们回回逃出来，赎金一个子儿也不用付。我们穿绿衣裳，因为那是草和叶子的颜色；我们往灌木底下一坐，或往草里一躺，他们打旁边走过去，瞧也瞧不见。」

===Original===
“Will you let me see your crock of gold?” said Seumas.

The Leprecaun looked at him fixedly for a moment.

“Do you like griddle bread and milk?” said he.

“I like it well,” Seumas answered.

“Then you had better have some,” and the Leprecaun took a piece of griddle bread from the shelf and filled two saucers with milk.

While the children were eating the Leprecauns asked them many questions﻿—

===Chinese===
「能让我看看你们的金罐吗？」谢默斯说。

矮妖定定地看了他一会儿。

「你爱吃烤饼（griddle bread）泡牛奶吗？」他说。

「爱吃得很。」谢默斯答道。

「那你最好吃一点。」矮妖从架子上拿了一块烤饼，又倒满两碟牛奶。

孩子们吃着，矮妖们问长问短——

===Original===
“What time do you get up in the morning?”

“Seven o’clock,” replied Seumas.

“And what do you have for breakfast?”

“Stirabout and milk,” he replied.

“It’s good food,” said the Leprecaun. “What do you have for dinner?”

“Potatoes and milk,” said Seumas.

“It’s not bad at all,” said the Leprecaun. “And what do you have for supper?”

Brigid answered this time because her brother’s mouth was full.

“Bread and milk, sir,” said she.

“There’s nothing better,” said the Leprecaun.

“And then we go to bed,” continued Brigid.

“Why wouldn’t you?” said the Leprecaun.

===Chinese===
「早上你们几点起床？」

「七点。」谢默斯答。

「早饭吃什么？」

「燕麦糊和牛奶。」他答。

「是好东西。」矮妖说。「午饭吃什么？」

「土豆和牛奶。」谢默斯说。

「一点也不赖。」矮妖说。「晚饭吃什么？」

这回轮到布里吉德回答，因为她哥哥嘴里塞得满满的。

「面包和牛奶，先生。」她说。

「再好不过了。」矮妖说。

「然后我们就上床睡觉。」布里吉德接着说。

「怎么不上呢？」矮妖说。

===Original===
It was at this point the Thin Woman of Inis Magrath knocked on the tree trunk and demanded that the children should be returned to her.

When she had gone away the Leprecauns held a consultation, whereat it was decided that they could not afford to anger the Thin Woman and the Shee of Croghan Conghaile, so they shook hands with the children and bade them goodbye. The Leprecaun who had enticed them away from home brought them back again, and on parting he begged the children to visit Gort na Cloca Mora whenever they felt inclined.

“There’s always a bit of griddle bread or potato cake, and a noggin of milk for a friend,” said he.

“You are very kind, sir,” replied Seumas, and his sister said the same words.

As the Leprecaun walked away they stood watching him.

“Do you remember,” said Seumas, “the way he hopped and waggled his leg the last time he was here?”

“I do so,” replied Brigid.

“Well, he isn’t hopping or doing anything at all this time,” said Seumas.

“He’s not in good humour tonight,” said Brigid, “but I like him.”

“So do I,” said Seumas.

When they went into the house the Thin Woman of Inis Magrath was very glad to see them, and she baked a cake with currants in it, and also gave them both stirabout and potatoes; but the Philosopher did not notice that they had been away at all. He said at last that “talking was bad wit, that women were always making a fuss, that children should be fed, but not fattened, and that beds were meant to be slept in.” The Thin Woman replied, “that he was a grisly old man without bowels, that she did not know what she had married him for, that he was three times her age, and that no one would believe what she had to put up with.”

===Chinese===
正在这时，伊尼斯马格拉的瘦女人敲响了树干，要求把孩子们还给她。

她走后，矮妖们合议了一番，议定实在开罪不起瘦女人和克罗根康盖尔的希神族，便同两个孩子握了手，道了别。当初把他们引出来的那个矮妖又把他们送了回去，临别时恳请孩子们高兴了就来戈特纳克洛卡莫拉玩。

「给朋友的烤饼或土豆饼总有一块，牛奶总有一小木杯（noggin）。」他说。

「您太好了，先生。」谢默斯答道，他妹妹也说了同样的话。

矮妖往回走，两人站着望他的背影。

「你记得吗，」谢默斯说，「上回他在这儿，又是蹦又是晃腿的那个样子？」

「记得清楚。」布里吉德答。

「可这一回，他既不蹦，也不来那么一下。」谢默斯说。

「他今晚不痛快。」布里吉德说，「可我喜欢他。」

「我也是。」谢默斯说。

他们进屋时，伊尼斯马格拉的瘦女人见了他们高兴得很，烤了一块加醋栗的饼，还给他们两人燕麦糊和土豆吃；哲学家呢，压根儿没觉出他们出门去过。他末了发表意见说：「多话是坏智慧；女人总在瞎咋呼；孩子该喂饱，但不该喂肥；床么，本来就是拿来睡的。」瘦女人回敬说：「他是个没心肝的凶老头；她不明白当初嫁他图什么；他比她大三倍年纪；她受的这份罪，说出去没人肯信。」

### IX / 第九章

===Original===
Pursuant to his arrangement with Meehawl MacMurrachu, the Philosopher sent the children in search of Pan. He gave them the fullest instructions as to how they should address the Sylvan Deity, and then, having received the admonishments of the Thin Woman of Inis Magrath, the children departed in the early morning.

When they reached the clearing in the pine wood, through which the sun was blazing, they sat down for a little while to rest in the heat. Birds were continually darting down this leafy shaft, and diving away into the dark wood. These birds always had something in their beaks. One would have a worm, or a snail, or a grasshopper, or a little piece of wool torn off a sheep, or a scrap of cloth, or a piece of hay; and when they had put these things in a certain place they flew up the sun-shaft again and looked for something else to bring home. On seeing the children each of the birds waggled his wings, and made a particular sound. They said “caw” and “chip” and “twit” and “tut” and “what” and “pit”; and one, whom the youngsters liked very much, always said “tit-tit-tit-tit-tit.” The children were fond of him because he was so all-of-a-sudden. They never knew where he was going to fly next, and they did not believe he knew himself. He would fly backwards and forwards, and up and down, and sideways and bawways—all, so to speak, in the one breath. He did this because he was curious to see what was happening everywhere, and, as something is always happening everywhere, he was never able to fly in a straight line for more than the littlest distance. He was a cowardly bird too, and continually fancied that some person was going to throw a stone at him from behind a bush, or a wall, or a tree, and these imaginary dangers tended to make his journeyings still more wayward and erratic. He never flew where he wanted to go himself, but only where God directed him, and so he did not fare at all badly.

===Chinese===
依照与米霍尔·麦克穆拉赫的约定，哲学家打发孩子们去寻找潘。他就该如何拜见这位林中神祇，对他们作了最详尽的叮嘱；随后，孩子们又领受了伊尼斯马格拉的瘦女人的一番训诫，便于清晨动身了。

他们走到松林中那片空地时，太阳正烈烈地照透林间，他们便坐下歇了一会儿。鸟儿们不断地顺着这条绿叶的光柱俯冲下来，又一头扎进幽暗的树林。这些鸟嘴里总叼着东西：这只叼着一条虫、一只蜗牛或一只蚱蜢，那只叼着一小片从羊身上撕下的羊毛、一块碎布或一截干草；它们把这些东西放到某个地方之后，又顺着光柱飞上去，再寻别的东西往家叼。一看见孩子们，每只鸟都摆动翅膀，发出各自的叫声。它们说「呱」「唧」「啾」「啧」「啥」「哔」；有一只孩子们顶喜欢的，总说「嘀嘀嘀嘀嘀」。孩子们喜欢它，就因为它行事全凭一时兴起。他们从不知道它下一刻要飞向哪里，也不信它自己知道。它一会儿向前一会儿向后，一会儿向上一会儿向下，一会儿横着一会儿斜着——可以说全在一口气里。它这样飞，是因为它好奇各处正发生着什么事；而各处总有事情在发生，所以它连最短的一段直线也飞不成。它还是只胆小的鸟，总疑心有人要从灌木后头、墙后头或树后头朝它扔石头，这些臆想出来的危险，使它的飞行愈发任性、愈发没个定准。它从来不照自己的意思飞，只照上天指引的方向飞，因此日子过得倒一点也不坏。

===Original===
The children knew each of the birds by their sounds, and always said these words to them when they came near. For a little time they had difficulty in saying the right word to the right bird, and sometimes said “chip” when the salutation should have been “tut.” The birds always resented this, and would scold them angrily, but after a little practice they never made any mistakes at all. There was one bird, a big, black fellow, who loved to be talked to. He used to sit on the ground beside the children, and say “caw” as long as they would repeat it after him. He often wasted a whole morning in talk, but none of the other birds remained for more than a few minutes at a time. They were always busy in the morning, but in the evening they had more leisure, and would stay and chat as long as the children wanted them. The awkward thing was that in the evening all the birds wanted to talk at the same moment, so that the youngsters never knew which of them to answer. Seumas Beg got out of that difficulty for a while by learning to whistle their notes, but, even so, they spoke with such rapidity that he could not by any means keep pace with them. Brigid could only whistle one note; it was a little flat “whoo” sound, which the birds all laughed at, and after a few trials she refused to whistle any more.

While they were sitting two rabbits came to play about in the brush. They ran round and round in a circle, and all their movements were very quick and twisty. Sometimes they jumped over each other six or seven times in succession, and every now and then they sat upright on their hind legs, and washed their faces with their paws. At other times they picked up a blade of grass, which they ate with great deliberation, pretending all the time that it was a complicated banquet of cabbage leaves and lettuce.

===Chinese===
孩子们凭叫声认得每一只鸟，鸟儿们靠近时，他们总对它们说这些话。起初有一阵子，他们难得把话对到该对的鸟身上——有时明明该道「啧」，却说成了「唧」。鸟儿们对这种事很不乐意，会气冲冲地数落他们；但稍加练习之后，他们就再没出过一丁点错。有一只鸟，是个黑黑的大块头，最爱有人跟它说话。它惯常蹲在孩子们身边的地上，只要他们肯跟着学，它就一个劲儿地说「呱」。它常常为一番闲谈耗掉整整一个上午，别的鸟却谁也不肯一次多待几分钟。早晨它们总是忙碌，到了傍晚便闲了些，孩子们想聊多久，它们就陪多久。难办的是，傍晚时分所有的鸟都想同时开口，小家伙们简直不知道该应哪一只的好。有一阵子，谢默斯·贝格学会了用口哨吹它们的音调，总算把这个难处应付了过去；可即便如此，它们说得太快，他怎么也跟不上。布里吉德只会吹一个音，是个扁扁的「呼」声，鸟儿们全笑话它，试了几回之后，她便再也不肯吹了。

他们坐着的时候，来了两只兔子，在灌木丛边玩耍。它们绕着圈儿跑，动作又快又迂回。有时它们接连互相跳过对方六七回，不时又后腿立起坐直，用前爪洗脸。还有的时候，它们叼起一片草叶，慢条斯理地嚼着，只当那是一席白菜叶和莴苣摆下的盛筵。

===Original===
While the children were playing with the rabbits an ancient, stalwart he-goat came prancing through the bracken. He was an old acquaintance of theirs, and he enjoyed lying beside them to have his forehead scratched with a piece of sharp stick. His forehead was hard as rock, and the hair grew there as sparse as grass does on a wall, or rather the way moss grows on a wall—it was a mat instead of a crop. His horns were long and very sharp, and brilliantly polished. On this day the he-goat had two chains around his neck—one was made of buttercups and the other was made of daisies, and the children wondered to each other who it was could have woven these so carefully. They asked the he-goat this question, but he only looked at them and did not say a word. The children liked examining this goat’s eyes; they were very big, and of the queerest light-gray colour. They had a strange steadfast look, and had also at times a look of queer, deep intelligence, and at other times they had a fatherly and benevolent expression, and at other times again, especially when he looked sidewards, they had a mischievous, light-and-airy, daring, mocking, inviting and terrifying look; but he always looked brave and unconcerned. When the he-goat’s forehead had been scratched as much as he desired he arose from between the children and went pacing away lightly through the wood. The children ran after him and each caught hold of one of his horns, and he ambled and reared between them while they danced along on his either side singing snatches of bird songs, and scraps of old tunes which the Thin Woman of Inis Magrath had learned among the people of the Shee.

===Chinese===
孩子们正跟兔子玩着，一头年高而雄壮的公羊踩着欢步从蕨丛中穿了出来。它是他们的老相识了，爱躺在他们身边，让他们拿一根尖树枝给它挠额头。它的额头硬得像石头，那上头的毛长得稀稀落落，如同墙上长草——不如说像墙上长苔——是一片垫子，而非一丛庄稼。它的角又长又尖，磨得雪亮。这一天，公羊脖子上挂着两条链子：一条是毛茛花编的，一条是雏菊编的。孩子们彼此纳闷，不知是谁把它们编得这样细巧。他们拿这话问公羊，它只望了望他们，一言不发。孩子们爱察看这只羊的眼睛：那眼睛很大，是顶古怪的浅灰色，透着一种奇异的坚定；有时又露出一种古怪而深沉的慧黠；有时是一副慈父般的和善神气；还有的时候——尤其是它侧过眼来看时——那眼神便又顽皮、又轻飘、又大胆、又嘲弄、又撩人、又骇人；但它看上去永远勇敢而满不在乎。公羊的额头挠足了，便从孩子们中间站起身，轻快地踱步进了树林。孩子们追上去，一人抓住它一只角，它在两人中间又颠又跳，他们则在它两侧踏着舞步，唱着鸟鸣的片段，唱着伊尼斯马格拉的瘦女人从希神族子民那里学来的旧调子。

===Original===
In a little time they came to Gort na Cloca Mora, but here the he-goat did not stop. They went past the big tree of the Leprecauns, through a broken part of the hedge and into another rough field. The sun was shining gloriously. There was scarcely a wind at all to stir the harsh grasses. Far and near was silence and warmth, an immense, cheerful peace. Across the sky a few light clouds sailed gently on a blue so vast that the eye failed before that horizon. A few bees sounded their deep chant, and now and again a wasp rasped hastily on his journey. Than these there was no sound of any kind. So peaceful, innocent and safe did everything appear that it might have been the childhood of the world as it was of the morning.

===Chinese===
不多时，他们到了戈特纳克洛卡莫拉，可公羊在这儿并没停下。他们走过矮妖们的大树，从树篱的一个破口钻出去，进了另一片荒草起伏的田野。阳光灿烂地照着。几乎没有一丝风来搅动粗硬的草叶。远近都是静谧与暖意，一片浩大而欢畅的安宁。几朵轻云横过天空，缓缓航行在那样辽阔的蔚蓝里——天际太远，目光未及穷尽便先倦了。几只蜜蜂嗡着深沉的吟诵，一只黄蜂偶尔在旅途上急促地磨响翅膀。除此之外，万籁无声。一切都显得这般平和、无辜而安稳，让人恍惚觉得这里不只是一日之晨，更是世界本身的童年。

===Original===
The children, still clinging to the friendly goat, came near the edge of the field, which here sloped more steeply to the mountain top. Great boulders, slightly covered with lichen and moss, were strewn about, and around them the bracken and gorse were growing, and in every crevice of these rocks there were plants whose little, tightfisted roots gripped a desperate, adventurous habitation in a soil scarcely more than half an inch deep. At some time these rocks had been smitten so fiercely that the solid granite surfaces had shattered into fragments. At one place a sheer wall of stone, ragged and battered, looked harshly out from the thin vegetation. To this rocky wall the he-goat danced. At one place there was a hole in the wall covered by a thick brush. The goat pushed his way behind this growth and disappeared. Then the children, curious to see where he had gone, pushed through also. Behind the bush they found a high, narrow opening, and when they had rubbed their legs, which smarted from the stings of nettles, thistles and gorse prickles, they went into the hole which they thought was a place the goat had for sleeping in on cold, wet nights. After a few paces they found the passage was quite comfortably big, and then they saw a light, and in another moment they were blinking at the god Pan and Caitilin Ni Murrachu.

===Chinese===
孩子们仍旧抓着这只友善的羊，来到田野边上；这里的地势更陡地倾向山顶。巨大的圆石上覆着薄薄一层地衣与青苔，四处散布；蕨草与荆豆在石间生长，每一条岩缝里都有草木扎根，它们小小的、攥得紧紧的根，在深不过半寸的泥土里，死死抓住一处奋不顾身的、冒险的栖身之所。在某个久远的时刻，这些岩石曾遭过何等猛烈的击打，连坚实的花岗岩表面都迸裂成了碎片。有一处，一堵陡直的石壁嶙峋残破，从稀疏的植被间冷峻地探出身来。公羊一路舞到这堵石壁前。壁上有个洞口，被一丛浓密的灌木遮着。公羊从这丛草木后头挤了进去，不见了。孩子们好奇它去了哪里，也跟着挤了进去。灌木后面是一道又高又窄的开口，他们揉了揉被荨麻、蓟刺和荆豆扎得生疼的腿，钻进了洞里，心想这大概是公羊在又冷又湿的夜里睡觉的地方。走了几步，他们发觉通道竟相当宽敞舒适，随后看见一点光亮，转眼间，他们已眨着眼睛，站在了牧神潘和卡蒂琳·尼·麦克穆拉赫面前。

===Original===
Caitilin knew them at once and came forward with welcome.

“O, Seumas Beg,” she cried reproachfully, “how dirty you have let your feet get. Why don’t you walk in the grassy places? And you, Brigid, have a right to be ashamed of yourself to have your hands the way they are. Come over here at once.”

Every child knows that every grown female person in the world has authority to wash children and to give them food; that is what grown people were made for, consequently Seumas and Brigid Beg submitted to the scouring for which Caitilin made instant preparation. When they were cleaned she pointed to a couple of flat stones against the wall of the cave and bade them sit down and be good, and this the children did, fixing their eyes on Pan with the cheerful gravity and curiosity which good-natured youngsters always give to a stranger.

Pan, who had been lying on a couch of dried grass, sat up and bent an equally cheerful regard on the children.

“Shepherd Girl,” said he, “who are those children?”

“They are the children of the Philosophers of Coilla Doraca; the Grey Woman of Dun Gortin and the Thin Woman of Inis Magrath are their mothers, and they are decent, poor children, God bless them.”

===Chinese===
卡蒂琳一眼认出了他们，迎上前来欢迎。

「哟，谢默斯·贝格，」她责备地喊道，「你的脚怎么弄得这么脏。为什么不走长草的地方？还有你，布里吉德，手弄成这副样子，真该害臊。马上过来。」

每个孩子都知道，世上一切成年女性都有权给孩子们洗脸、给他们吃的——大人就是为这个造出来的。因此谢默斯·贝格和布里吉德·贝格乖乖听从了卡蒂琳即刻着手准备的搓洗。洗净之后，她指着洞壁旁两块平整的石头，叫他们坐下乖乖待着。孩子们照办了，把眼睛盯着潘，带着好脾气的孩子对生人惯有的那种快活的郑重与好奇。

潘本来躺在一张干草铺就的榻上，这时坐起身，同样快活地打量起孩子们。

「牧羊女，」他说，「这两个孩子是谁？」

「他们是科拉多拉卡那几位哲学家的孩子；邓戈尔坦的灰衣女人和伊尼斯马格拉的瘦女人是他们的母亲。他们是好孩子，可怜的孩子，上帝保佑他们。」

===Original===
“What have they come here for?”

“You will have to ask themselves that.”

Pan looked at them smilingly.

“What have you come here for, little children?” said he.

===Chinese===
「他们来这儿做什么？」

「这你得问他们自己。」

潘笑吟吟地看着他们。

「小朋友们，你们来这儿做什么呀？」他说。

===Original===
The children questioned one another with their eyes to see which of them would reply, and then Seumas Beg answered:

“My father sent me to see you, sir, and to say that you were not doing a good thing in keeping Caitilin Ni Murrachu away from her own place.”

Brigid Beg turned to Caitilin—

“Your father came to see our father, and he said that he didn’t know what had become of you at all, and that maybe you were lying flat in a ditch with the black crows picking at your flesh.”

===Chinese===
孩子们用眼睛互相探问，看该由谁来回答，随后谢默斯·贝格开了口：

「先生，是我父亲叫我来见您的，还说，您把卡蒂琳·尼·麦克穆拉赫扣在这儿、不让她回自己的家，可不是在做好事。」

布里吉德·贝格转向卡蒂琳——

「你父亲来找过我父亲，他说他根本不知道你如今怎么样了，说不定你正直挺挺躺在沟里，让黑乌鸦啄你的肉呢。」

===Original===
“And what,” said Pan, “did your father say to that?”

“He told us to come and ask her to go home.”

“Do you love your father, little child?” said Pan.

Brigid Beg thought for a moment. “I don’t know, sir,” she replied.

“He doesn’t mind us at all,” broke in Seumas Beg, “and so we don’t know whether we love him or not.”

===Chinese===
「那么，」潘说，「你父亲听了怎么说？」

「他叫我们来请她回家去。」

「小朋友，你爱你的父亲吗？」潘说。

布里吉德·贝格想了一想。「我不知道，先生。」她答道。

「他压根儿不理会我们，」谢默斯·贝格插嘴道，「所以我们不知道爱不爱他。」

===Original===
“I like Caitilin,” said Brigid, “and I like you.”

“So do I,” said Seumas.

“I like you also, little children,” said Pan. “Come over here and sit beside me, and we will talk.”

So the two children went over to Pan and sat down one each side of him, and he put his arms about them. “Daughter of Murrachu,” said he, “is there no food in the house for guests?”

===Chinese===
「我喜欢卡蒂琳，」布里吉德说，「也喜欢你。」

「我也喜欢。」谢默斯说。

「我也喜欢你们，小朋友们，」潘说，「过来坐到我身边来，咱们说说话。」

于是两个孩子走到潘身边，在他两旁坐下，他伸臂搂住他们。「麦克穆拉赫的女儿，」他说，「家里没有款待客人的吃食吗？」

===Original===
“There is a cake of bread, a little goat’s milk and some cheese,” she replied, and she set about getting these things.

“I never ate cheese,” said Seumas. “Is it good?”

“Surely it is,” replied Pan. “The cheese that is made from goat’s milk is rather strong, and it is good to be eaten by people who live in the open air, but not by those who live in houses, for such people do not have any appetite. They are poor creatures whom I do not like.”

“I like eating,” said Seumas.

“So do I,” said Pan. “All good people like eating. Every person who is hungry is a good person, and every person who is not hungry is a bad person. It is better to be hungry than rich.”

===Chinese===
「有一块面包、一点羊奶，还有些奶酪。」她答道，随即动手去取这些东西。

「我从没吃过奶酪，」谢默斯说，「好吃吗？」

「当然好吃，」潘答道，「羊奶做的奶酪味道很冲。住在露天里的人吃了正好，住在屋子里的人吃了却不行，因为那种人没有任何胃口。他们是些可怜的家伙，我不喜欢。」

「我喜欢吃东西。」谢默斯说。

「我也喜欢，」潘说，「凡是好人都喜欢吃。肚子饿的人个个是好人，不饿的人个个是坏人。挨饿胜过富有。」

===Original===
Caitilin having supplied the children with food, seated herself in front of them. “I don’t think that is right,” said she. “I have always been hungry, and it was never good.”

“If you had always been full you would like it even less,” he replied, “because when you are hungry you are alive, and when you are not hungry you are only half alive.”

“One has to be poor to be hungry,” replied Caitilin. “My father is poor and gets no good of it but to work from morning to night and never to stop doing that.”

===Chinese===
卡蒂琳把吃食安顿给孩子们，在他们面前坐下。「我看这话说得不对，」她说，「我一直都在挨饿，可从来没有什么好处。」

「你要是一直都吃得饱饱的，就更不会喜欢了，」他答道，「因为人饿着的时候才是活着的，不饿的时候只是半活着。」

「人得先穷，才会挨饿，」卡蒂琳答道，「我父亲就穷，可穷没给他半点好处，只叫他从早干到黑，一刻也不得停。」

===Original===
“It is bad for a wise person to be poor,” said Pan, “and it is bad for a fool to be rich. A rich fool will think of nothing else at first but to find a dark house wherein to hide away, and there he will satisfy his hunger, and he will continue to do that until his hunger is dead and he is no better than dead but a wise person who is rich will carefully preserve his appetite. All people who have been rich for a long time, or who are rich from birth, live a great deal outside of their houses, and so they are always hungry and healthy.”

===Chinese===
「聪明人受穷是坏事，蠢人发财也是坏事。」潘说，「发了财的蠢人起初一心只想寻一所黑黢黢的房子把自己藏起来，在那儿填饱他的肚子，并且就这样一直下去，直到他的饥饿死去，他自己也活得同死人无异；发了财的聪明人却会小心翼翼地保住自己的胃口。凡是富了很久的人，或是生来就富的人，大半光阴都在自家宅子外头过活，所以他们总是又饿又健康。」

===Original===
“Poor people have no time to be wise,” said Caitilin.

“They have time to be hungry,” said Pan. “I ask no more of them.”

“My father is very wise,” said Seumas Beg.

“How do you know that, little boy?” said Pan.

“Because he is always talking,” replied Seumas.

===Chinese===
「穷人可没有功夫做聪明人。」卡蒂琳说。

「他们有功夫挨饿，」潘说，「我对他们的要求仅此而已。」

「我父亲就非常聪明。」谢默斯·贝格说。

「小家伙，这你怎么知道的？」潘说。

「因为他老是说话。」谢默斯答道。

===Original===
“Do you always listen, my dear?”

“No, sir,” said Seumas; “I go to sleep when he talks.”

“That is very clever of you,” said Pan.

“I go to sleep too,” said Brigid.

“It is clever of you also, my darling. Do you go to sleep when your mother talks?”

===Chinese===
「那你老是听着吗，亲爱的？」

「不，先生，」谢默斯说，「他一说话我就睡觉。」

「你这样做可真聪明。」潘说。

「我也睡觉。」布里吉德说。

「你也很聪明，小宝贝。你们母亲说话的时候，你也睡吗？」

===Original===
“Oh, no,” she answered. “If we went to sleep then our mother would pinch us and say that we were a bad breed.”

“I think your mother is wise,” said Pan. “What do you like best in the world, Seumas Beg?”

The boy thought for a moment and replied:

“I don’t know, sir.”

===Chinese===
「啊，那可不，」她答道，「那时候我们要是睡了，母亲就会拧我们，说我们是坏种。」

「我看你们的母亲是聪明的。」潘说，「谢默斯·贝格，这世界上你最喜欢什么？」

男孩想了一会儿，答道：

「我不知道，先生。」

===Original===
Pan also thought for a little time.

“I don’t know what I like best either,” said he. “What do you like best in the world, Shepherd Girl?”

Caitilin’s eyes were fixed on his.

“I don’t know yet,” she answered slowly.

===Chinese===
潘也想了一小会儿。

「我也不知道自己最喜欢什么，」他说，「牧羊女，这世界上你最喜欢什么？」

卡蒂琳的眼睛定定地望着他的眼睛。

「我还不知道。」她缓缓地答道。

===Original===
“May the gods keep you safe from that knowledge,” said Pan gravely.

“Why would you say that?” she replied. “One must find out all things, and when we find out a thing we know if it is good or bad.”

“That is the beginning of knowledge,” said Pan, “but it is not the beginning of wisdom.”

“What is the beginning of wisdom?”

===Chinese===
「愿众神保佑你，别让你知道那个。」潘正色道。

「你为什么这么说？」她答道，「人总得把样样事情都弄明白。一件事弄明白了，我们才知道它是好是坏。」

「那是知识的开端，」潘说，「却不是智慧的开端。」

「什么是智慧的开端？」

===Original===
“It is carelessness,” replied Pan.

“And what is the end of wisdom?” said she.

“I do not know,” he answered, after a little pause.

“Is it greater carelessness?” she enquired.

“I do not know, I do not know,” said he sharply. “I am tired of talking,” and, so saying, he turned his face away from them and lay down on the couch.

===Chinese===
「是漫不经心。」潘答道。

「那智慧的尽头又是什么？」她问。

「我不知道。」他略停了停，答道。

「是更深的漫不经心吗？」她又问。

「我不知道，我不知道。」他没好气地说道，「我说累了。」说着，他把脸从他们那边转开，在榻上躺下了。

===Original===
Caitilin in great concern hurried the children to the door of the cave and kissed them goodbye.

“Pan is sick,” said the boy gravely.

“I hope he will be well soon again,” the girl murmured.

“Yes, yes,” said Caitilin, and she ran back quickly to her lord.

===Chinese===
卡蒂琳满心忧虑，急忙把孩子们送到洞口，吻别了他们。

「潘病了。」男孩一本正经地说。

「我盼他快点好起来。」女孩低声说。

「会的，会的。」卡蒂琳说，随即快步跑回她的主人身边。

### X / 第十章

===Original===
When the children reached home they told the Philosopher the result of their visit. He questioned them minutely as to the appearance of Pan, how he had received them, and what he had said in defence of his iniquities; but when he found that Pan had not returned any answer to his message he became very angry. He tried to persuade his wife to undertake another embassy setting forth his abhorrence and defiance of the god, but the Thin Woman replied sourly that she was a respectable married woman, that having been already bereaved of her wisdom she had no desire to be further curtailed of her virtue, that a husband would go any length to asperse his wife’s reputation, and that although she was married to a fool her self-respect had survived even that calamity. The Philosopher pointed out that her age, her appearance, and her tongue were sufficient guarantees of immunity against the machinations of either Pan or slander, and that he had no personal feelings in the matter beyond a scientific and benevolent interest in the troubles of Meehawl MacMurrachu; but this was discounted by his wife as the malignant and subtle tactics customary to all husbands.

===Chinese===
孩子们到家后，把此行的结果告诉了哲学家。他细细追问潘的模样、他如何接待他们、又为自家劣迹作了什么辩白；待发觉潘对他的口信竟没有半句答复，他勃然大怒。他试图劝妻子再出一趟差，替他向那位神明转达他的深恶痛绝与公然对抗，瘦女人却没好气地回说：她是个正经的已婚妇人；智慧既已被剥夺，可不想连德行也再遭削减；做丈夫的为了败坏妻子的名声，什么事都干得出来；何况她嫁的虽是个傻子，她的自尊心连这场灾难都挺过来了。哲学家指出，她的年纪、她的相貌、还有她那张嘴，足以担保她既不怕潘的算计，也不怕谗言的中伤；况且他在此事上并无半点私心，不过是出于对米霍尔·麦克穆拉赫困境的一种科学与慈善并重的关怀罢了。他的妻子对这套话嗤之以鼻，只当它是天下丈夫人人惯用的阴险伎俩。

===Original===
Matters appeared to be thus at a deadlock so far as they were immediately concerned, and the Philosopher decided that he would lay the case before Angus Óg and implore his protection and assistance on behalf of the Clann MacMurrachu. He therefore directed the Thin Woman to bake him two cakes of bread, and set about preparations for a journey.

The Thin Woman baked the cakes, and put them in a bag, and early on the following morning the Philosopher swung this bag over his shoulder, and went forth on his quest.

===Chinese===
就眼前的形势而言，事情似乎就此僵住了。哲学家决定把这桩案子禀呈安格斯·奥格，为麦克穆拉赫氏族恳求他的庇护与援助。于是他吩咐瘦女人给他烙两块面饼，自己着手打点行装。

瘦女人烙好了饼，装进一只袋子。第二天一早，哲学家把袋子往肩上一甩，出门寻访去了。

===Original===
When he came to the edge of the pine wood he halted for a few moments, not being quite certain of his bearings, and then went forward again in the direction of Gort na Cloca Mora. It came into his mind as he crossed the Gort that he ought to call on the Leprecauns and have a talk with them, but a remembrance of Meehawl MacMurrachu and the troubles under which he laboured (all directly to be traced to the Leprecauns) hardened his heart against his neighbours, so that he passed by the yew tree without any stay. In a short time he came to the rough, heather-clumped field wherein the children had found Pan, and as he was proceeding up the hill, he saw Caitilin Ni Murrachu walking a little way in front with a small vessel in her hand. The she-goat which she had just milked was bending again to the herbage, and as Caitilin trod lightly in front of him the Philosopher closed his eyes in virtuous anger and opened them again in a not unnatural curiosity, for the girl had no clothes on. He watched her going behind the brush and disappearing in the cleft of the rock, and his anger, both with her and Pan, mastering him he forsook the path of prudence which soared to the mountain top, and followed that leading to the cave. The sound of his feet brought Caitilin out hastily, but he pushed her by with a harsh word. “Hussy,” said he, and he went into the cave where Pan was.

===Chinese===
走到松林边上，他停了片刻，一时拿不准方向，随后又朝戈特纳克洛卡莫拉的方向走去。穿过那片戈特时，他忽然想起应当去拜访一下矮妖们，同他们谈谈；可一想起米霍尔·麦克穆拉赫和他正陷其中的祸事（件件都能直接追到矮妖们头上），他对邻人们的心便硬了起来，于是从紫杉树旁径直走过，没有停留。不多时，他来到孩子们寻见潘的那片石楠丛生的野田。他正往山上走，看见卡蒂琳·尼·麦克穆拉赫走在前面不远的地方，手里拿着一只小罐。她刚挤过奶的那只母羊又低头啃起草来。卡蒂琳轻盈地走在他前面，哲学家出于道义的愤怒闭上了眼睛，又出于并不奇怪的好奇睁了开来——因为那姑娘身上一丝不挂。他望着她走到灌木后面，消失在岩石的裂口里；他对她、也对潘的怒火压倒了他，于是他撇下那条通往山顶的谨慎之路，改走通往山洞的那条。他的脚步声让卡蒂琳慌忙迎了出来，他却一句话把她推搡过去。「贱丫头。」他说着，径直走进潘所在的洞里。

===Original===
As he went in he already repented of his harshness and said—

“The human body is an aggregation of flesh and sinew, around a central bony structure. The use of clothing is primarily to protect this organism from rain and cold, and it may not be regarded as the banner of morality without danger to this fundamental premise. If a person does not desire to be so protected who will quarrel with an honourable liberty? Decency is not clothing but Mind. Morality is behaviour. Virtue is thought—”

“I have often fancied,” he continued to Pan, whom he was now confronting, “that the effect of clothing on mind must be very considerable, and that it must have a modifying rather than an expanding effect, or, even, an intensifying as against an exuberant effect. With clothing the whole environment is immediately affected. The air, which is our proper medium, is only filtered to our bodies in an abated and niggardly fashion which can scarcely be as beneficial as the generous and unintermitted elemental play. The question naturally arises whether clothing is as unknown to nature as we have fancied? Viewed as a protective measure against atmospheric rigour we find that many creatures grow, by their own central impulse, some kind of exterior panoply which may be regarded as their proper clothing. Bears, cats, dogs, mice, sheep and beavers are wrapped in fur, hair, fell, fleece or pelt, so these creatures cannot by any means be regarded as being naked. Crabs, cockroaches, snails and cockles have ordered around them a crusty habiliment, wherein their original nakedness is only to be discovered by force, and other creatures have similarly provided themselves with some species of covering. Clothing, therefore, is not an art, but an instinct, and the fact that man is born naked and does not grow his clothing upon himself from within but collects it from various distant and haphazard sources is not any reason to call this necessity an instinct for decency. These, you will admit, are weighty reflections and worthy of consideration before we proceed to the wide and thorny subject of moral and immoral action. Now, what is virtue?”—

===Chinese===
一进门他就为自己的粗暴后悔了，说道——

「人体是血肉与筋腱的聚合，包在一副居中的骨骼结构之外。衣物的用途，首要在于保护这具机体免受风雨寒冷；倘若不顾这一根本前提，把它当作道德的旗帜，便是危险的事。一个人若不愿受这样的保护，谁又会去指责这份体面的自由呢？体面不在衣着，而在心念。道德是行为。德性是思想——」

「我常常想，」他继续对潘说——这时他已面对着潘了——「衣着对心念的影响必定十分可观，而且必定是一种收敛的、而非舒展的影响，或者甚至是一种强化内敛、压制张扬的影响。有了衣着，整个环境立刻就变了样。空气本是我们天然的媒介，如今却只能以衰减的、吝啬的方式滤到我们身上，这怎么也比不上丰沛的、不间断的自然元素直接抚拂来得有益。于是自然生出一个问题：衣着当真如我们所以为的那样，是大自然闻所未闻的吗？若把它看作抵御严酷气候的防护手段，我们会发现许多生物都出于自身内在的冲动，长出了某种外在的甲装，那便可以视为它们本分的衣着。熊、猫、狗、鼠、绵羊与河狸都裹在毛、发、皮、绒或裘里，因此决不能说这些生物是赤裸的。螃蟹、蟑螂、蜗牛和鸟蛤周身布置了一层硬壳的衣甲，除非诉诸强力，简直发现不了它们原本的赤裸；别的生物也都同样为自己备下了某种覆盖。可见衣着不是技艺，而是本能。人生而赤裸，衣裳并非从体内、从自己身上生长出来，而是从各处遥远的、碰运气的来源搜集而来——这一事实并不构成任何理由，能把这种必需唤作体面的本能。这些，你定会承认，都是分量沉重的思考，值得我们在进入道德与非道德行为这个广大而多刺的论题之前好好掂量。那么，什么是德性？」——

===Original===
Pan, who had listened with great courtesy to these remarks, here broke in on the Philosopher.

“Virtue,” said he, “is the performance of pleasant actions.”

The Philosopher held the statement for a moment on his forefinger.

“And what, then, is vice?” said he.

“It is vicious,” said Pan, “to neglect the performance of pleasant actions.”

===Chinese===
潘极有礼貌地听完了这些议论，这时打断了哲学家。

「德性，」他说，「就是做愉快的事情。」

哲学家把这句话在食指上托了片刻。

「那么，」他说，「什么是罪恶呢？」

「疏于做愉快的事情，」潘说，「便是罪恶。」

===Original===
“If this be so,” the other commented, “philosophy has up to the present been on the wrong track.”

“That is so,” said Pan. “Philosophy is an immoral practice because it suggests a standard of practice impossible of being followed, and which, if it could be followed, would lead to the great sin of sterility.”

“The idea of virtue,” said the Philosopher, with some indignation, “has animated the noblest intellects of the world.”

“It has not animated them,” replied Pan; “it has hypnotised them so that they have conceived virtue as repression and self-sacrifice as an honourable thing instead of the suicide which it is.”

===Chinese===
「倘若当真如此，」对方评道，「那么哲学直至今日都是走上了岔路。」

「正是如此，」潘说，「哲学是一种不道德的行当，因为它提出了一套无人能行的行为标准；这套标准纵然行得通，也会导向那桩大罪——不结果实。」

「德性的观念，」哲学家带着几分愤慨说，「曾激励过世上最高贵的那些心智。」

「那不是激励，」潘答道，「那是催眠。他们被催眠了，才把德性构想成压抑，把自我牺牲当作光荣的事——殊不知那分明是自杀。」

===Original===
“Indeed,” said the Philosopher; “this is very interesting, and if it is true the whole conduct of life will have to be very much simplified.”

“Life is already very simple,” said Pan; “it is to be born and to die, and in the interval to eat and drink, to dance and sing, to marry and beget children.”

“But it is simply materialism,” cried the Philosopher.

“Why do you say ‘but’?” replied Pan.

===Chinese===
「的确，」哲学家说，「这很有意思。倘若这是真的，整个人生的经营之道都得大大简化了。」

「人生本来就很简单，」潘说，「无非是生下来，死去，中间这段时光里吃喝、跳舞、唱歌、结婚、生孩子。」

「可这纯粹是唯物主义。」哲学家喊道。

「你为什么要说『可是』呢？」潘答道。

===Original===
“It is sheer, unredeemed animalism,” continued his visitor.

“It is any name you please to call it,” replied Pan.

“You have proved nothing,” the Philosopher shouted.

“What can be sensed requires no proof.”

===Chinese===
「这是彻头彻尾、无可救药的兽性主义。」来客继续说道。

「你爱叫它什么，它就是什么。」潘答道。

「你什么也没有证明。」哲学家嚷道。

「能被感知的东西，无需证明。」

===Original===
“You leave out the new thing,” said the Philosopher. “You leave out brains. I believe in mind above matter. Thought above emotion. Spirit above flesh.”

“Of course you do,” said Pan, and he reached for his oaten pipe.

The Philosopher ran to the opening of the passage and thrust Caitilin aside. “Hussy,” said he fiercely to her, and he darted out.

As he went up the rugged path he could hear the pipes of Pan, calling and sobbing and making high merriment on the air.

===Chinese===
「你漏掉了那个新东西，」哲学家说，「你漏掉了头脑。我相信心智高于物质，思想高于情感，灵魂高于肉体。」

「你当然是信的。」潘说着，伸手去拿他的燕麦笛。

哲学家奔到通道口，把卡蒂琳推到一边。「贱丫头。」他冲她狠狠地说，随即窜了出去。

他顺着崎岖的小路上山时，还听得见潘的笛声，在空中呼唤、呜咽，又扬起高高的欢腾。

### XI / 第十一章

===Original===
“She does not deserve to be rescued,” said the Philosopher, “but I will rescue her. Indeed,” he thought a moment later, “she does not want to be rescued, and, *therefore*, I will rescue her.”

As he went down the road her shapely figure floated before his eyes as beautiful and simple as an old statue. He wagged his head angrily at the apparition, but it would not go away. He tried to concentrate his mind on a deep, philosophical maxim, but her disturbing image came between him and his thought, blotting out the latter so completely that a moment after he had stated his aphorism he could not remember what it had been. Such a condition of mind was so unusual that it bewildered him.

“Is a mind, then, so unstable,” said he, “that a mere figure, an animated geometrical arrangement can shake it from its foundations?”

The idea horrified him: he saw civilisation building its temples over a volcano …

“A puff,” said he, “and it is gone. Beneath all is chaos and red anarchy, over all a devouring and insistent appetite. Our eyes tell us what to think about, and our wisdom is no more than a catalogue of sensual stimuli.”

===Chinese===
「她不配被搭救，」哲学家说，「可我偏要救她。说实在的，」过了一会儿他又想道，「她并不想被搭救，正*因此*，我更要救她。」

顺着路走下去时，她那窈窕的身影浮现在他眼前，美得像一尊古代雕像一样简洁。他气冲冲地朝这幻影摇头，它却不肯散去。他试着把心思凝在一条深邃的哲学格言上，可她那扰人的形象偏偏横在他与思想之间，把后者涂抹得干干净净——格言出口才一分钟，他就想不起方才说的是什么了。这种心境如此罕见，把他自己也弄糊涂了。

「难道心智就这样不稳，」他说，「单凭一个身形，一具会动的几何构图，就能把它从根基上撼动？」

这念头叫他心惊：他看见文明把庙宇盖在一座火山上……

「一口气，」他说，「就把它吹没了。底下是混沌与赤色的无序，上头是饕餮般纠缠不休的胃口。我们的眼睛吩咐我们该想什么，我们的智慧不过是一份感官刺激的清单。」

===Original===
He would have been in a state of deep dejection were it not that through his perturbation there bubbled a stream of such amazing well-being as he had not felt since childhood. Years had toppled from his shoulders. He left one pound of solid matter behind at every stride. His very skin grew flexuous, and he found a pleasure in taking long steps such as he could not have accounted for by thought. Indeed, thought was the one thing he felt unequal to, and it was not precisely that he could not think but that he did not want to. All the importance and authority of his mind seemed to have faded away, and the activity which had once belonged to that organ was now transferred to his eyes. He saw, amazedly, the sunshine bathing the hills and the valleys. A bird in the hedge held him—beak, head, eyes, legs, and the wings that tapered widely at angles to the wind. For the first time in his life he really saw a bird, and one minute after it had flown away he could have reproduced its strident note. With every step along the curving road the landscape was changing. He saw and noted it almost in an ecstasy. A sharp hill jutted out into the road, it dissolved into a sloping meadow, rolled down into a valley and then climbed easily and peacefully into a hill again. On this side a clump of trees nodded together in the friendliest fashion. Yonder a solitary tree, well-grown and clean, was contented with its own bright company. A bush crouched tightly on the ground as though, at a word, it would scamper from its place and chase rabbits across the sward with shouts and laughter. Great spaces of sunshine were everywhere, and everywhere there were deep wells of shadow; and the one did not seem more beautiful than the other. That sunshine! Oh, the glory of it, the goodness and bravery of it, how broadly and grandly it shone, without stint, without care; he saw its measureless generosity and gloried in it as though himself had been the flinger of that largesse. And was he not? Did the sunlight not stream from his head and life from his fingertips? Surely the well-being that was in him did bubble out to an activity beyond the universe. Thought! Oh! the petty thing! but motion! emotion! these were the realities. To feel, to do, to stride forward in elation chanting a paean of triumphant life!

After a time he felt hungry, and thrusting his hand into his wallet he broke off a piece of one of his cakes and looked about for a place where he might happily eat it. By the side of the road there was a well; just a little corner filled with water. Over it was a rough stone coping, and around, hugging it on three sides almost from sight, were thick, quiet bushes. He would not have noticed the well at all but for a thin stream, the breadth of two hands, which tiptoed away from it through a field. By this well he sat down and scooped the water in his hand and it tasted good.

===Chinese===
若不是在这番纷乱底下一直冒着一般自童年以来从未有过的奇异的身心舒泰，他早该陷入深深的沮丧了。岁月从他的肩头纷纷跌落。他每跨一步，就在身后甩下一磅沉重的物质。连他的皮肤都变得柔韧起来，他迈开大步时竟感到一种快乐，是思想无从解释的那种快乐。的确，思想正是他自觉无力应付的一样东西；倒不是说他不能想，而是他不想想。他的心智原先具有的那一切重要与威严仿佛都消退了，那个器官一度专司的活动，如今都转移到他的眼睛上。他惊讶地看见阳光浴着群山与河谷。树篱间的一只鸟就勾住了他——喙、头、眼睛、腿，还有迎着风斜斜张开去的双翼。他有生以来第一次真正看见了一只鸟；它飞走一分钟之后，他还能学出它那尖利的叫声。顺着蜿蜒的路每走一步，风景都在变换。他几乎是心醉神迷地看着它、记着它。一座陡峭的山嘴探进路里，随即化作一面斜坡草场，滚落进一道山谷，又从容安详地重新爬成一座山。这一边，一丛树木以最亲热的姿态一齐点头。那边一棵孤树，长得端正干净，独与自己明亮的身影为伴，也自满足。一丛灌木紧紧伏在地上，仿佛只要一声令下，就会窜出原地，喊叫着笑着追兔子跑过草地。到处是大片大片的阳光，到处是深深的光影之井；而这一者并不比那一者更美。那阳光！啊，它的荣光，它的善意与豪勇，它照得多阔、多堂皇，不吝惜，也无牵挂；他看见它那无量的慷慨，为之欢欣，仿佛那场挥霍正是他自己撒出去的。难道不是吗？阳光难道不是从他头顶倾泻、生命难道不是从他指尖流出？千真万确，他身内的舒泰正汩汩冒出，涌成一股超出宇宙的活动。思想！嗐，那点小东西！而运动！情感！这些才是实在。去感受，去做，欢欣鼓舞地大步向前，唱一支得胜生命的凯歌！

走了一阵他饿了，便把手伸进褡裢，掰下一块饼，四下张望找个能快活吃饼的地方。路边有一眼井；不过是小小一汪水。井口圈着粗糙的石沿，四周浓密安静的灌木几乎从三面把它抱了个严实。若不是有一股两掌宽的细流踮着脚尖从井里穿过田野溜走，他根本不会留意这眼井。他在井边坐下，掬水来喝，水很甘甜。

===Original===
He was eating his cake when a sound touched his ear from some distance, and shortly a woman came down the path carrying a vessel in her hand to draw water. She was a big, comely woman, and she walked as one who had no misfortunes and no misgivings. When she saw the Philosopher sitting by the well she halted a moment in surprise and then came forward with a good-humoured smile.

“Good morrow to you, sir,” said she.

“Good morrow to you too, ma’am,” replied the Philosopher. “Sit down beside me here and eat some of my cake.”

“Why wouldn’t I, indeed,” said the woman, and she did sit beside him.

The Philosopher cracked a large piece off his cake and gave it to her and she ate some.

“There’s a taste on that cake,” said she. “Who made it?”

“My wife did,” he replied.

“Well, now!” said she, looking at him. “Do you know, you don’t look a bit like a married man.”

“No?” said the Philosopher.

“Not a bit. A married man looks comfortable and settled: he looks finished, if you understand me, and a bachelor looks unsettled and funny, and he always wants to be running round seeing things. I’d know a married man from a bachelor any day.”

===Chinese===
他正吃着饼，一阵声响从远处传到耳边。不多时，一个女人提着打水的罐子顺小路走来。她是个高大标致的女人，走路的模样像是一生没有遭过灾难、也没有过疑惧。她看见哲学家坐在井边，惊讶地顿了一顿，随即带着和气的微笑走上前来。

「先生，您好。」她说。

「您也好，太太，」哲学家答道，「坐到我旁边来，尝尝我的饼。」

「那有什么不肯的。」女人说着，当真在他身边坐下了。

哲学家从饼上掰下一大块给她，她吃了一些。

「这饼上有股味道，」她说，「谁做的？」

「我妻子做的。」他答道。

「哎呀，」她打量着他说，「您知道吗，您一点也不像个结了婚的男人。」

「不像吗？」哲学家说。

「一点也不像。结了婚的男人看着舒坦、安顿；说得明白点，他看着像个成品。单身汉呢，看着没着没落、滑稽，老想着东跑西逛看热闹。随便挑哪一天，我都分得出结了婚的和没结婚的。」

===Original===
“How would you know that?” said the Philosopher.

“Easily,” said she, with a nod. “It’s the way they look at a woman. A married man looks at you quietly as if he knew all about you. There isn’t any strangeness about him with a woman at all; but a bachelor man looks at you very sharp and looks away and then looks back again, the way you’d know he was thinking about you and didn’t know what you were thinking about him; and so they are always strange, and that’s why women like them.”

“Why!” said the Philosopher, astonished, “do women like bachelors better than married men?”

“Of course they do,” she replied heartily. “They wouldn’t look at the side of the road a married man was on if there was a bachelor man on the other side.”

“This,” said the Philosopher earnestly, “is very interesting.”

“And the queer thing is,” she continued, “that when I came up the road and saw you I said to myself ‘it’s a bachelor man.’ How long have you been married, now?”

“I don’t know,” said the Philosopher. “Maybe it’s ten years.”

“And how many children would you have, mister?”

“Two,” he replied, and then corrected himself, “No, I have only one.”

“Is the other one dead?”

“I never had more than one.”

===Chinese===
「您怎么分得出呢？」哲学家说。

「容易，」她点点头说，「看他们瞧女人的眼神就知道了。结了婚的男人瞧你，安安静静的，好像你的底细他全知道；他跟女人相处一点不生分。单身汉呢，很尖地瞧你一眼，赶紧挪开，又瞟回来，叫你一看就知道他在琢磨你，却不知道你怎么琢磨他；所以他们身上总有一股生分劲儿，女人们爱的就是这个。」

「哎呀！」哲学家吃惊地说，「女人竟更喜欢单身汉，不喜欢结了婚的男人？」

「那当然，」她爽快地答道，「路这边要是站着个结了婚的，那边站着个单身汉，女人连这边看都不会看一眼。」

「这，」哲学家恳切地说，「非常有意思。」

「更怪的是，」她接着说，「我顺着路刚走过来，一眼看见您，就对自己说：这是个单身汉。——您结婚多少年啦？」

「不知道，」哲学家说，「总有十年了吧。」

「那您有几个孩子呢，先生？」

「两个，」他答道，随即又改口，「不对，我只有一个。」

「另一个死了吗？」

「我从来就只有一个。」

===Original===
“Ten years married and only one child,” said she. “Why, man dear, you’re not a married man. What were you doing at all, at all! I wouldn’t like to be telling you the children I have living and dead. But what I say is that married or not you’re a bachelor man. I knew it the minute I looked at you. What sort of a woman is herself?”

“She’s a thin sort of woman,” cried the Philosopher, biting into his cake.

“Is she now?”

“And,” the Philosopher continued, “the reason I talked to you is because you are a fat woman.”

“I am not fat,” was her angry response.

“You are fat,” insisted the Philosopher, “and that’s the reason I like you.”

“Oh, if you mean it that way …” she chuckled.

“I think,” he continued, looking at her admiringly, “that women ought to be fat.”

“Tell you the truth,” said she eagerly, “I think that myself. I never met a thin woman but she was a sour one, and I never met a fat man but he was a fool. Fat women and thin men; it’s nature,” said she.

“It is,” said he, and he leaned forward and kissed her eye.

“Oh, you villain!” said the woman, putting out her hands against him.

The Philosopher drew back abashed.

“Forgive me,” he began, “if I have alarmed your virtue—”

“It’s the married man’s word,” said she, rising hastily: “now I know you; but there’s a lot of the bachelor in you all the same, God help you! I’m going home.” And, so saying, she dipped her vessel in the well and turned away.

“Maybe,” said the Philosopher, “I ought to wait until your husband comes home and ask his forgiveness for the wrong I’ve done him.”

The woman turned round on him and each of her eyes was as big as a plate.

“What do you say?” said she. “Follow me if you dare and I’ll set the dog on you; I will so,” and she strode viciously homewards.

After a moment’s hesitation the Philosopher took his own path across the hill.

===Chinese===
「结了十年婚，就一个孩子，」她说，「哎呀亲爱的，您可不算个结了婚的男人。您这些年到底是干什么的呀！我那些孩子，活的死的一大串，我都不好意思跟您数。可我还是要说，不管结没结婚，您就是个单身汉。我瞧您一眼就知道了。——您家里那位是个什么样的女人？」

「是个瘦精精的女人。」哲学家咬着饼说。

「是吗？」

「还有，」哲学家接着说，「我之所以跟您说话，就因为您是个胖女人。」

「我不胖。」她气冲冲地回道。

「您就是胖，」哲学家一口咬定，「我就是为这个喜欢您。」

「哦，您要是这个意思……」她嗤嗤笑了。

「我看，」他接着说，爱慕地望着她，「女人就该胖。」

「跟您说真话，」她起劲地说，「我自己也这么想。我见过的瘦女人，没一个不酸；见过的胖男人，没一个不傻。女人要胖，男人要瘦；这是天性。」

「正是。」他说着，凑过去亲了亲她的眼睛。

「哟，你这个坏东西！」女人说着，伸手去推他。

哲学家窘迫地缩了回来。

「原谅我，」他开口道，「倘若我惊扰了您的贞操——」

「这可是结了婚的男人说的话，」她说着赶忙站起身来，「这下我可认清您了；不过您骨子里还是有很多单身汉的气，上帝保佑您！我要回家了。」说着，她把罐子浸到井里打了水，转身走了。

「也许，」哲学家说，「我该等您丈夫回来，请他原谅我对不起他的地方。」

那女人猛地回过身来，两只眼睛瞪得像盘子一样大。

「你说什么？」她说，「你敢跟着我，我就放狗咬你；我说到做到。」她气势汹汹地大步往家去了。

哲学家迟疑了片刻，取自己的路翻山去了。

===Original===
The day was now well advanced, and as he trudged forward the happy quietude of his surroundings stole into his heart again and so toned down his recollection of the fat woman that in a little time she was no more than a pleasant and curious memory. His mind was exercised superficially, not in thinking, but in wondering how it was he had come to kiss a strange woman. He said to himself that such conduct was not right; but this statement was no more than the automatic working of a mind long exercised in the distinctions of right and wrong, for, almost in the same breath, he assured himself that what he had done did not matter in the least. His opinions were undergoing a curious change. Right and wrong were meeting and blending together so closely that it became difficult to dissever them, and the obloquy attaching to the one seemed out of proportion altogether to its importance, while the other by no means justified the eulogy wherewith it was connected. Was there any immediate or even distant, effect on life caused by evil which was not instantly swung into equipoise by goodness? But these slender reflections troubled him only for a little time. He had little desire for any introspective quarryings. To feel so well was sufficient in itself. Why should thought be so apparent to us, so insistent? We do not know we have digestive or circulatory organs until these go out of order, and then the knowledge torments us. Should not the labours of a healthy brain be equally subterranean and equally competent? Why have we to think aloud and travel laboriously from syllogism to ergo, chary of our conclusions and distrustful of our premises? Thought, as we know it, is a disease and no more. The healthy mentality should register its convictions and not its labours. Our ears should not hear the clamour of its doubts nor be forced to listen to the pro and con wherewith we are eternally badgered and perplexed.

===Chinese===
这时天色已过晌午，他一路跋涉，四下里欢畅的宁静又悄悄渗进他心里，把那胖女人的影子冲淡了；不多时，她便只剩一段愉快而古怪的回忆。他的心思浮在面上活动着——不是思考，只是纳闷自己怎么会去亲了一个陌生女人。他对自己说，这种行为是不对的；可这话不过是一颗久于分辨是非的心在自动运转罢了，因为几乎在同一口气里，他又让自己相信，他做的这件事压根儿无关紧要。他的种种见解正在经历一场古怪的变化。对与错正越靠越近、越融越紧，简直难解难分；恶所挨的骂，同它的重要性比起来全然不成比例；善呢，也实在配不上与它相连的那份颂扬。恶对生活造成的任何眼下或久远的影响，哪一样不是立刻就被善摆平了？但这些细细的念头只烦扰了他一小会儿。他没有多大兴致去做任何内省式的挖掘。感觉这样好，本身就已经足够。思想为什么对我们这样显豁、这样纠缠不休？消化器官和循环器官不出毛病，我们根本不知道自己有它们；一旦出了毛病，那点知识就来折磨我们了。一颗健康的大脑，它的工作难道不也该同样在地下进行、同样胜任愉快吗？为什么我们非得把想的过程说给自己听，从三段论辛辛苦苦走到结论，既吝惜结论、又不信前提？思想，照我们现在这个样子，不过是一种病。健康的心智该登记它的定见，而不是它的劳作。我们的耳朵不该听见它的疑虑吵嚷，也不该被迫去听那些永远缠磨我们、搅昏我们的正反两面。

===Original===
The road was winding like a ribbon in and out of the mountains. On either side there were hedges and bushes—little, stiff trees which held their foliage in their hands and dared the winds snatch a leaf from that grip. The hills were swelling and sinking, folding and soaring on every view. Now the silence was startled by the falling tinkle of a stream. Far away a cow lowed, a long, deep monotone, or a goat’s call trembled from nowhere to nowhere. But mostly there was a silence which buzzed with a multitude of small winged life. Going up the hills the Philosopher bent forward to the gradient, stamping vigorously as he trod, almost snorting like a bull in the pride of successful energy. Coming down the slope he braced back and let his legs loose to do as they pleased. Didn’t they know their business?—Good luck to them, and away!

As he walked along he saw an old woman hobbling in front of him. She was leaning on a stick and her hand was red and swollen with rheumatism. She hobbled by reason of the fact that there were stones in her shapeless boots. She was draped in the sorriest miscellaneous rags that could be imagined, and these were knotted together so intricately that her clothing, having once been attached to her body, could never again be detached from it. As she walked she was mumbling and grumbling to herself, so that her mouth moved round and round in an india rubber fashion.

===Chinese===
道路像一条丝带，在群山间绕进绕出。两旁是树篱与灌木——一株株矮而硬挺的小树，把满捧的叶子攥在手里，任风来抢，也抢不去一片。山峦随处起伏，一折一扬。寂静不时被一道溪水的泠泠坠落惊起。远处一声牛哞，长而深沉的单音；或是一声羊鸣，从无处颤向无处。但多半时候是一种寂静——一种被无数带翅膀的小生命嗡嗡充盈着的寂静。上坡时，哲学家躬身向着坡度，踏步生风，几乎像一头劲头得逞的公牛似的喷着鼻息。下坡时他便向后绷住，放开两腿任它们自行其是。它们还不清楚自己的本分吗？——祝它们好运，撒腿去吧！

正走着，他看见一个老妇人一瘸一拐地在前面挪。她拄着一根棍子，手因风湿又红又肿。她之所以瘸拐，是因为那双没了形状的靴子里尽是石子。她身上披挂着想象得到的最寒酸的、五花八门的破布，破布彼此结得那样繁复，以致她这身衣裳一旦上了身，就永远再也解不下来了。她一边走一边自言自语地咕哝抱怨，嘴巴像橡皮一样一圈一圈地动。

===Original===
The Philosopher soon caught up on her.

“Good morrow, ma’am,” said he.

But she did not hear him: she seemed to be listening to the pain which the stones in her boots gave her.

“Good morrow, ma’am,” said the Philosopher again.

This time she heard him and replied, turning her old, bleared eyes slowly in his direction—

“Good morrow to yourself, sir,” said she, and the Philosopher thought her old face was a very kindly one.

“What is it that is wrong with you, ma’am?” said he.

“It’s my boots, sir,” she replied. “Full of stones they are, the way I can hardly walk at all, God help me!”

“Why don’t you shake them out?”

“Ah, sure, I couldn’t be bothered, sir, for there are so many holes in the boots that more would get in before I could take two steps, and an old woman can’t be always fidgeting, God help her!”

There was a little house on one side of the road, and when the old woman saw this place she brightened up a little.

“Do you know who lives in that house?” said the Philosopher.

“I do not,” she replied, “but it’s a real nice house with clean windows and a shiny knocker on the door, and smoke in the chimney—I wonder would herself give me a cup of tea now if I asked her—A poor old woman walking the roads on a stick! and maybe a bit of meat, or an egg perhaps. …”

“You could ask,” suggested the Philosopher gently.

“Maybe I will, too,” said she, and she sat down by the road just outside the house and the Philosopher also sat down.

===Chinese===
哲学家很快追上了她。

「您好，太太。」他说。

可她没听见：她似乎正专心听着靴子里的石子给她的疼痛。

「您好，太太。」哲学家又说了一遍。

这一回她听见了，慢慢转过那双昏花的老眼望着他，答道——

「也祝您好，先生。」她说。哲学家觉得她那张老脸十分和善。

「您这是怎么了，太太？」他说。

「是我的靴子，先生，」她答道，「里头全是石子，弄得我几乎走不动路，上帝救我！」

「那为什么不把石子倒出来呢？」

「哎，说真的，我懒得弄了，先生，靴子上窟窿太多，倒净了不出两步又灌进来，老太婆哪能时时刻刻折腾，上帝可怜她吧！」

路边有一座小房子，老妇人望见这地方，精神略略一振。

「您知道这房子里住的是谁吗？」哲学家说。

「不知道，」她答道，「不过这房子可真体面，窗子擦得干干净净，门上还装着亮闪闪的门环，烟囱里冒着烟——不知我开口讨杯茶喝，那位太太肯不肯给——一个拄着棍子走路的穷老婆子！兴许还能讨着一点肉，或者一个鸡蛋。……」

「您可以问问看。」哲学家温和地提了一句。

「兴许我真去问呢。」她说着，就在那房子外头的路边坐了下来，哲学家也坐下了。

===Original===
A little puppy dog came from behind the house and approached them cautiously. Its intentions were friendly but it had already found that amicable advances are sometimes indifferently received, for, as it drew near, it wagged its dubious tail and rolled humbly on the ground. But very soon the dog discovered that here there was no evil, for it trotted over to the old woman, and without any more preparation jumped into her lap.

The old woman grinned at the dog—

“Ah, you thing you!” said she, and she gave it her finger to bite. The delighted puppy chewed her bony finger, and then instituted a mimic warfare against a piece of rag that fluttered from her breast, barking and growling in joyous excitement, while the old woman fondled and hugged it.

The door of the house opposite opened quickly, and a woman with a frostbitten face came out.

“Leave that dog down,” said she.

The old woman grinned humbly at her.

“Sure, ma’am, I wouldn’t hurt the little dog, the thing!”

“Put down that dog,” said the woman, “and go about your business—the likes of you ought to be arrested.”

A man in shirt sleeves appeared behind her, and at him the old woman grinned even more humbly.

“Let me sit here for a while and play with the little dog, sir,” said she; “sure the roads do be lonesome—”

The man stalked close and grabbed the dog by the scruff of the neck. It hung between his finger and thumb with its tail tucked between its legs and its eyes screwed round on one side in amazement.

“Be off with you out of that, you old strap!” said the man in a terrible voice.

So the old woman rose painfully to her feet again, and as she went hobbling along the dusty road she began to cry.

The Philosopher also arose; he was very indignant but did not know what to do. A singular lassitude also prevented him from interfering. As they paced along his companion began mumbling, more to herself than to him—

===Chinese===
一只小狗从屋后转出来，小心地朝他们走近。它来意友善，却早已领教过好意的接近有时并不受欢迎，所以离得近了，它摇着一条拿不准主意的尾巴，卑怯地就地打滚。但很快小狗就发现这里没有恶意，便小跑到老妇人跟前，也不再多做什么铺垫，一纵身跳进了她的怀里。

老妇人朝狗咧嘴笑了——

「啊，你这个小东西！」她把手指递给它咬。小狗欢喜地啃着她那根瘦骨嶙峋的手指，随后又对她胸前一飘一飘的破布条发起了一场假打仗，又吠又呜地闹得欢天喜地，老妇人则一路抚它抱它。

对面的屋门倏地开了，出来一个脸冻得铁青的女人。

「把那狗放下来。」她说。

老妇人朝她谦卑地赔笑。

「真的，太太，我不会伤着这小狗的，这小东西！」

「把狗放下，」那女人说，「该干什么干什么去——你们这号人就该抓起来。」

一个只穿衬衫的男人出现在她身后，老妇人冲他笑得愈发谦卑。

「让我在这儿坐一会儿，逗逗这小狗吧，先生，」她说，「路上实在太冷清了——」

那男人大步逼近，一把攥住小狗的颈皮。小狗吊在他的拇指与食指之间，尾巴夹在两腿中间，眼睛惊愕地斜吊向一边。

「快滚，你这老货！」男人声音凶得吓人。

老妇人只得又痛苦地站起身来，她顺着尘土飞扬的路一瘸一拐地走着，哭了起来。

哲学家也站了起来；他义愤填膺，却不知如何是好。一种说不出的倦怠也让他无法插手。两人并排走着，同伴开始咕咕哝哝，与其说是对他说，不如说是对自己说——

===Original===
“Ah, God be with me,” said she, “an old woman on a stick, that hasn’t a place in the wide world to go to or a neighbour itself. … I wish I could get a cup of tea, so I do. I wish to God I could get a cup of tea. … Me sitting down in my own little house, with the white tablecloth on the table, and the butter in the dish, and the strong, red tea in the teacup; and me pouring cream into it, and, maybe, telling the children not to be wasting the sugar, the things! and himself saying he’d got to mow the big field today, or that the red cow was going to calve, the poor thing! and that if the boys went to school, who was going to weed the turnips—and me sitting drinking my strong cup of tea, and telling him where that old trapesing hen was laying. … Ah, God be with me! an old creature hobbling along the roads on a stick. I wish I was a young girl again, so I do, and himself coming courting me, and him saying that I was a real nice little girl surely, and that nothing would make him happy or easy at all but me to be loving him.—Ah, the kind man that he was, to be sure, the kind, decent man. … And Sorca Reilly to be trying to get him from me, and Kate Finnegan with her bold eyes looking after him in the Chapel; and him to be saying that along with me they were only a pair of old nanny goats. … And then me to be getting married and going home to my own little house with my man—ah, God be with me! and him kissing me, and laughing, and frightening me with his goings-on. Ah, the kind man, with his soft eyes, and his nice voice, and his jokes and laughing, and him thinking the world and all of me—ay, indeed. … And the neighbours to be coming in and sitting round the fire in the nighttime, putting the world through each other, and talking about France and Russia and them other queer places, and him holding up the discourse like a learned man, and them all listening to him and nodding their heads at each other, and wondering at his education and all: or, maybe, the neighbours to be singing, or him making me sing the Coulin, and him to be proud of me … and then him to be killed on me with a cold on his chest. … Ah, then, God be with me, a lone, old creature on a stick, and the sun shining into her eyes and she thirsty—I wish I had a cup of tea, so I do. I wish to God I had a cup of tea and a bit of meat … or, maybe, an egg. A nice fresh egg laid by the speckeldy hen that used to be giving me all the trouble, the thing! … Sixteen hens I had, and they were the ones for laying, surely. … It’s the queer world, so it is, the queer world—and the things that do happen for no reason at all. … Ah, God be with me! I wish there weren’t stones in my boots, so I do, and I wish to God I had a cup of tea and a fresh egg. Ah, glory be, my old legs are getting tireder every day, so they are. Wisha, one time—when himself was in it—I could go about the house all day long, cleaning the place, and feeding the pigs, and the hens and all, and then dance half the night, so I could: and himself proud of me. …”

The old woman turned up a little rambling road and went on still talking to herself, and the Philosopher watched her go up that road for a long time. He was very glad she had gone away, and as he tramped forward he banished her sad image so that in a little time he was happy again. The sun was still shining, the birds were flying on every side, and the wide hillside above him smiled gaily.

===Chinese===
「啊，上帝可怜我，」她说，「一个拄棍子的老太婆，全世界连个落脚的地方都没有，连个邻人都没有。……我多想喝杯茶啊，真的。我求上帝给我一杯茶喝吧。……我坐在自家的小房子里，桌上铺着白桌布，碟子里是黄油，茶杯里是酽酽的红茶；我往里兑着奶油，也许还嘱咐孩子们别糟蹋糖，这些小东西！他说他今天得去割大草地，或者说红母牛要下犊了，可怜的家伙！还说男孩子们要是都上学去了，萝卜地谁去薅草——我坐着喝我那杯酽茶，告诉他那只爱到处乱窜的老母鸡这会儿在哪儿下蛋。……啊，上帝可怜我！一个老东西拄着棍子在路上挪。我真想再当一回年轻姑娘，真的；他来向我求爱，说我真是个顶可人的小姑娘，说这世上唯有我爱他，他才能快活、才能安心。——啊，他是个多好的人啊，真的，那么和气、那么正派。……索卡·赖利（Sorca Reilly）想把他从我身边抢走，凯特·芬尼根（Kate Finnegan）那双泼辣的眼睛还在礼拜堂里追着他瞟；他就说，跟我一比，她们不过是两只老母羊。……后来我们成了亲，我跟着我的男人回了自家的小屋——啊，上帝可怜我！他吻我，笑闹，他那疯劲儿吓得我心里乱跳。啊，那个好心的男人，眼睛那么温柔，嗓音那么好听，会逗趣、爱笑，把我看得比整个世界还重——真的，真的。……夜里邻人们进门来，围着火坐下，彼此把世界翻来覆去地数落，谈论法兰西、俄罗斯和那些别的怪地方；他像个有学问的人似的把话头撑起来，大伙儿都听他说，彼此点着头，惊叹他的学问：有时候呢，是邻居们唱歌，或者他叫我唱《库林》（the Coulin），他为我骄傲……再后来，一场风寒落在胸口，他就这么撇下我死了。……啊，上帝可怜我，一个孤零零的老东西拄着棍子，太阳照进她的眼睛，她口渴——我真想喝杯茶，真的。我求上帝给我一杯茶、一点肉吧……或者，一个鸡蛋也好。一只新鲜鸡蛋，要那只花斑母鸡下的——就是从前尽给我惹麻烦的那只，这个家伙！……我养过十六只母鸡，只只都是下蛋的好手，真的。……这世界真古怪，真的，古怪得很——多少事情毫无来由地就发生了。……啊，上帝可怜我！我真希望靴子里没有石子，真的；我求上帝给我一杯茶、一只新鲜鸡蛋。啊，荣耀归于主，我这两条老腿一天比一天乏了，真的。想当年——他还在的时候——我能整日在屋里屋外忙个不停，收拾屋子，喂猪喂鸡，到了夜里还能跳半宿的舞，真的：他还为我骄傲呢。……」

老妇人拐上一条弯弯曲曲的小路，嘴里仍旧念叨着走远了。哲学家久久望着她走上那条路的背影。她走了，他很欢喜；他一路跋涉，把她的愁容赶出脑海，不多时便又快活起来。太阳还照着，鸟儿四面飞着，头顶宽阔的山坡欢快地微笑。

===Original===
A small, narrow road cut at right angles into his path, and as he approached this he heard the bustle and movement of a host, the trample of feet, the rolling and creaking of wheels, and the long unwearied drone of voices. In a few minutes he came abreast of this small road, and saw an ass and cart piled with pots and pans, and walking beside this there were two men and a woman. The men and the woman were talking together loudly, even fiercely, and the ass was drawing his cart along the road without requiring assistance or direction. While there was a road he walked on it: when he might come to a cross road he would turn to the right: when a man said “whoh” he would stop: when he said “hike” he would go backwards, and when he said “yep” he would go on again. That was life, and if one questioned it, one was hit with a stick, or a boot, or a lump of rock: if one continued walking nothing happened, and that was happiness.

The Philosopher saluted this cavalcade.

“God be with you,” said he.

“God and Mary be with you,” said the first man.

“God, and Mary, and Patrick be with you,” said the second man.

“God, and Mary, and Patrick, and Brigid be with you,” said the woman.

The ass, however, did not say a thing. As the word “whoh” had not entered into the conversation he knew it was none of his business, and so he turned to the right on the new path and continued his journey.

===Chinese===
一条狭窄的小路与他走的路成直角相交。走近时，他听见一群人的喧嚷与骚动：杂沓的脚步，车轮的滚动与吱呀，还有那不见疲倦的长长的嗡嗡人语。几分钟后他走到这条小路口，看见一头驴拉着一辆堆满锅锅罐罐的车，车旁走着两男一女。男人和女人高声交谈，简直算得上激烈；驴子则不用任何人帮衬指点，自己顺着路拉车。有路，它就顺着走；到了岔路口，它就往右转；人喊一声「吁」，它就停下；喊「退」，它就倒退；喊「驾」，它就接着走。这就是生活；谁若质问它，谁就要挨棍子、挨靴子、挨石头；谁若只管往前走，就什么事也没有——那就是幸福。

哲学家向这行人马致意。

「愿上帝与你们同在。」他说。

「愿上帝和圣母与您同在。」第一个男人说。

「愿上帝、圣母和帕特里克与您同在。」第二个男人说。

「愿上帝、圣母、帕特里克，还有布里吉德与您同在。」女人说。

驴子却一言不发。谈话里没有出现「吁」这个字，它便知道这事与它无干，于是朝新路右边一转，继续赶它的路去了。

===Original===
“Where are you going to, stranger,” said the first man.

“I am going to visit Angus Óg,” replied the Philosopher.

The man gave him a quick look.

“Well,” said he, “that’s the queerest story I ever heard. Listen here,” he called to the others, “this man is looking for Angus Óg.”

The other man and woman came closer.

“What would you be wanting with Angus Óg, Mister Honey?” said the woman.

“Oh,” replied the Philosopher, “it’s a particular thing, a family matter.”

There was silence for a few minutes, and they all stepped onwards behind the ass and cart.

“How do you know where to look for himself?” said the first man again: “maybe you got the place where he lives written down in an old book or on a carved stone?”

“Or did you find the staff of Amergin or of Ossian in a bog and it written from the top to the bottom with signs?” said the second man.

“No,” said the Philosopher, “it isn’t that way you’d go visiting a god. What you do is, you go out from your house and walk straight away in any direction with your shadow behind you so long as it is towards a mountain, for the gods will not stay in a valley or a level plain, but only in high places; and then, if the god wants you to see him, you will go to his rath as direct as if you knew where it was, for he will be leading you with an airy thread reaching from his own place to wherever you are, and if he doesn’t want to see you, you will never find out where he is, not if you were to walk for a year or twenty years.”

===Chinese===
「您上哪儿去，外乡人？」第一个男人说。

「我去拜访安格斯·奥格。」哲学家答道。

那男人飞快地瞟了他一眼。

「嗬，」他说，「这可是我一辈子听过的最古怪的事。听着，」他招呼另外两个，「这个人要找安格斯·奥格。」

另一个男人和女人凑了过来。

「您找安格斯·奥格想做什么呢，蜜糖先生（Mister Honey）？」女人说。

「哦，」哲学家答道，「是件特别的事，一桩家务。」

沉默了几分钟，众人跟在驴车后面一齐往前走。

「您怎么知道该上哪儿找他呢？」第一个男人又说，「莫非您在一本旧书或一块刻石上抄下了他住的地方？」

「还是您在泥炭沼里捡到了阿默金（Amergin）或奥西安（Ossian）的手杖，上上下下写满了符记？」第二个男人说。

「不是，」哲学家说，「拜访神可不是这么个走法。你只消走出家门，让影子拖在身后，朝着山，随便哪个方向一直走；因为神不肯住在山谷或平川，只肯住在高处。然后，倘若那位神愿意见你，你自会径直走到他的古堡（rath），仿佛你原本就知道它在哪儿——因为他会用一根虚无缥缈的线，从他的居处一路牵到你脚下，引着你走；倘若他不愿见你，你就永远打听不出他住在哪儿，哪怕走上一年，走上二十年。」

===Original===
“How do you know he wants to see you?” said the second man.

“Why wouldn’t he want?” said the Philosopher.

“Maybe, Mister Honey,” said the woman, “you are a holy sort of a man that a god would like well.”

“Why would I be that?” said the Philosopher. “The gods like a man whether he’s holy or not if he’s only decent.”

“Ah, well, there’s plenty of that sort,” said the first man. “What do you happen to have in your bag, stranger?”

“Nothing,” replied the Philosopher, “but a cake and a half that was baked for my journey.”

“Give me a bit of your cake, Mister Honey,” said the woman. “I like to have a taste of everybody’s cake.”

“I will, and welcome,” said the Philosopher.

“You may as well give us all a bit while you are about it,” said the second man. “That woman hasn’t got all the hunger of the world.”

“Why not,” said the Philosopher, and he divided the cake.

===Chinese===
「那您怎么知道他愿意见您呢？」第二个男人说。

「他为什么不愿意呢？」哲学家说。

「兴许，蜜糖先生，」女人说，「您是位圣洁的人，神见了就喜欢。」

「我为什么要是那种人？」哲学家说，「神喜欢一个人，不管他圣洁不圣洁，只要他正派就行。」

「哈，那这种人可多了去了，」第一个男人说，「您袋子里碰巧装着什么呀，外乡人？」

「没什么，」哲学家答道，「一块半饼，是为我上路烙的。」

「给我一块尝尝吧，蜜糖先生，」女人说，「我爱尝每个人的饼。」

「给，别客气。」哲学家说。

「干脆趁便给我们每人一块吧，」第二个男人说，「这世上的饿又不是全叫您一个人占了。」

「有什么不可。」哲学家说着，把饼分了。

===Original===
“There’s a sup of water up yonder,” said the first man, “and it will do to moisten the cake—Whoh, you devil,” he roared at the ass, and the ass stood stock still on the minute.

There was a thin fringe of grass along the road near a wall, and towards this the ass began to edge very gently.

“Hike, you beast, you,” shouted the man, and the ass at once hiked, but he did it in a way that brought him close to the grass. The first man took a tin can out of the cart and climbed over the little wall for water. Before he went he gave the ass three kicks on the nose, but the ass did not say a word, he only hiked still more which brought him directly on to the grass, and when the man climbed over the wall the ass commenced to crop the grass. There was a spider sitting on a hot stone in the grass. He had a small body and wide legs, and he wasn’t doing anything.

“Does anybody ever kick you in the nose?” said the ass to him.

“Ay does there,” said the spider; “you and your like that are always walking on me, or lying down on me, or running over me with the wheels of a cart.”

“Well, why don’t you stay on the wall?” said the ass.

“Sure, my wife is there,” replied the spider.

“What’s the harm in that?” said the ass.

“She’d eat me,” said the spider, “and, anyhow, the competition on the wall is dreadful, and the flies are getting wiser and timider every season. Have you got a wife yourself, now?”

“I have not,” said the ass; “I wish I had.”

“You like your wife for the first while,” said the spider, “and after that you hate her.”

“If I had the first while I’d chance the second while,” replied the ass.

“It’s bachelor’s talk,” said the spider; “all the same, we can’t keep away from them,” and so saying he began to move all his legs at once in the direction of the wall. “You can only die once,” said he.

“If your wife was an ass she wouldn’t eat you,” said the ass.

“She’d be doing something else then,” replied the spider, and he climbed up the wall.

The first man came back with the can of water and they sat down on the grass and ate the cake and drank the water. All the time the woman kept her eyes fixed on the Philosopher.

===Chinese===
「上头那儿有一口水，正好润润饼——吁，你这个魔鬼。」第一个男人朝驴子吼了一声，驴子应声站定，一秒不差。

路边靠墙的地方有一线细草，驴子开始极其斯文地朝那边蹭。
