「退，你这畜生。」男人喝道，驴子立刻照退，可退的路数偏偏让它又贴近了草边。第一个男人从车上取出一只铁皮罐，翻过矮墙去打水。走之前他照着驴鼻子踢了三脚，驴子一声不吭，只是接着再退——这一退可好，四蹄正正退到了草上；等男人翻过墙去，驴子便埋头吃起草来。草地上有一只蜘蛛蹲在一块晒热的石头上。它身子小，腿脚张得开，什么也没干。

「可有人踢过你的鼻子？」驴子问它。

「可不有嘛，」蜘蛛说，「你和你们这一路的，不是踩我就是压我，再不然拿车轮从我身上碾过去。」

「那你干嘛不待在墙上？」驴子说。

「嗐，我老婆在那儿呢。」蜘蛛答道。

「那有什么不好？」驴子说。

「她会吃了我，」蜘蛛说，「再说墙上的竞争也可怕，苍蝇一季比一季精、一季比一季胆小。——你自己可有老婆？」

「没有，」驴子说，「倒真想有一个。」

「起先一阵子你喜欢老婆，」蜘蛛说，「往后就恨她。」

「只要有起先那一阵子，往后那一阵子我认赌服输。」驴子答道。

「这是单身汉的口气，」蜘蛛说，「话虽如此，我们还是离不开她们。」说着，它一齐挪动所有的腿，朝墙的方向去了。「人只能死一回。」它说。

「你老婆要是头驴，她就不会吃你了。」驴子说。

「那她就会干别的了。」蜘蛛答道，爬上了墙头。

第一个男人提着一罐水回来了，他们在草地上坐下，吃饼喝水。那女人自始至终眼睛都盯在哲学家身上。

===Original===
“Mister Honey,” said she, “I think you met us just at the right moment.”

The other two men sat upright and looked at each other and then with equal intentness they looked at the woman.

“Why do you say that?” said the Philosopher.

“We were having a great argument along the road, and if we were to be talking from now to the day of doom that argument would never be finished.”

“It must have been a great argument. Was it about predestination or where consciousness comes from?”

“It was not; it was which of these two men was to marry me.”

“That’s not a great argument,” said the Philosopher.

“Isn’t it,” said the woman. “For seven days and six nights we didn’t talk about anything else, and that’s a great argument or I’d like to know what is.”

“But where is the trouble, ma’am?” said the Philosopher.

“It’s this,” she replied, “that I can’t make up my mind which of the men I’ll take, for I like one as well as the other and better, and I’d as soon have one as the other and rather.”

“It’s a hard case,” said the Philosopher.

“It is,” said the woman, “and I’m sick and sorry with the trouble of it.”

“And why did you say that I had come up in a good minute?”

“Because, Mister Honey, when a woman has two men to choose from she doesn’t know what to do, for two men always become like brothers so that you wouldn’t know which of them was which: there isn’t any more difference between two men than there is between a couple of hares. But when there’s three men to choose from, there’s no trouble at all; and so I say that it’s yourself I’ll marry this night and no one else—and let you two men be sitting quiet in your places, for I’m telling you what I’ll do and that’s the end of it.”

“I’ll give you my word,” said the first man, “that I’m just as glad as you are to have it over and done with.”

“Moidered I was,” said the second man, “with the whole argument, and the this and that of it, and you not able to say a word but—maybe I will and maybe I won’t, and this is true and that is true, and why not to me and why not to him—I’ll get a sleep this night.”

The Philosopher was perplexed.

“You cannot marry me, ma’am,” said he, “because I’m married already.”

The woman turned round on him angrily.

“Don’t be making any argument with me now,” said she, “for I won’t stand it.”

The first man looked fiercely at the Philosopher, and then motioned to his companion.

“Give that man a clout in the jaw,” said he.

The second man was preparing to do this when the woman intervened angrily.

“Keep your hands to yourself,” said she, “or it’ll be the worse for you. I’m well able to take care of my own husband,” and she drew nearer and sat between the Philosopher and the men.

At that moment the Philosopher’s cake lost all its savour, and he packed the remnant into his wallet. They all sat silently looking at their feet and thinking each one according to his nature. The Philosopher’s mind, which for the past day had been in eclipse, stirred faintly to meet these new circumstances, but without much result. There was a flutter at his heart which was terrifying, but not unpleasant. Quickening through his apprehension was an expectancy which stirred his pulses into speed. So rapidly did his blood flow, so quickly were an hundred impressions visualized and recorded, so violent was the surface movement of his brain that he did not realize he was unable to think and that he was only seeing and feeling.

The first man stood up.

“The night will be coming on soon,” said he, “and we had better be walking on if we want to get a good place to sleep. Yep, you devil,” he roared at the ass, and the ass began to move almost before he lifted his head from the grass. The two men walked one on either side of the cart, and the woman and the Philosopher walked behind at the tailboard.

===Chinese===
「蜜糖先生，」她说，「我觉得您来得正是时候。」

另外两个男人直起身子，彼此对望一眼，随即又同样专注地望着那女人。

「您为什么这么说？」哲学家问。

「我们路上正吵着一桩天大的争执，就算从现在吵到审判日，也吵不出个完。」

「那一定是场大争执。是关于先定的宿命，还是意识从何而来？」

「都不是；是这两个男人哪一个该娶我。」

「这算不得大争执。」哲学家说。

「怎么不算，」女人说，「七天六夜，我们没谈过别的；这要不是大争执，我倒想知道什么是。」

「那为难在哪儿呢，太太？」哲学家说。

「为难就为难在，」她答道，「这两个男人我拿不定主意要哪一个：这个我喜欢得跟那个一样多，又更多一点；要这个我跟要那个一样甘愿，还更甘愿一点。」

「这案子难办。」哲学家说。

「是难办，」女人说，「我为这个又愁又苦。」

「那您方才为什么说我来得正是时候？」

「因为呀，蜜糖先生，一个女人面前要是只有两个男人好挑，她就没了主张：两个男人处着处着总变得跟兄弟似的，你都分不清谁是谁；两个男人之间的分别，比两只野兔之间的还小。可要是面前有三个男人好挑，那就半点也不难了。所以我说，今夜我要嫁的就是您，别人一概不要——你们两个男人给我安安静静坐好，我把要说的话说了，事情就这么定了。」

「我向你担保，」第一个男人说，「这事能了结，我跟你们一样高兴。」

「我可被这场争论折磨惨了，」第二个男人说，「翻来覆去，没完没了；你呢，除了『也许肯也许不肯』『这也对那也对』『凭啥给他不凭啥给我』，一句囫囵话都说不出来——今夜我可算能睡个觉了。」

哲学家慌了神。

「您不能嫁给我，太太，」他说，「因为我已经有妻子了。」

女人气冲冲地朝他转过身来。

「这会儿你别跟我辩，」她说，「我不吃这一套。」

第一个男人凶狠地瞪着哲学家，然后朝同伴递了个眼色。

「给这家伙下巴一拳。」他说。

第二个男人正要动手，女人怒气冲冲地拦在中间。

「把你们的手收好，」她说，「不然有你们好看的。我自己丈夫我自己护得住。」她凑近前来，坐在哲学家和两个男人中间。

就在那一刻，哲学家手里的饼彻底没了滋味，他把剩下的收进褡裢。众人默默坐着，各看各的脚尖，各按各的天性出神。哲学家那颗近一日来黯然失色的心智，微微一动，来应付这新的处境，却没想出什么名堂。他心头一阵扑腾，骇人，却并不难受。穿过惊惶，一股期待正在升腾，把他的脉搏催得飞快。血流得那样急，上百种印象那样快地被看见、被记下，大脑表层的活动那样剧烈，以致他没有意识到自己已经不能思考——他只是在看，在感觉。

第一个男人站起身。

「天快黑了，」他说，「想找个好地方过夜，就得赶紧走。——驾，你这个魔鬼。」他朝驴子吼道，驴子还没把头从草里抬起来，就几乎先动了身。两个男人一左一右走在车旁，女人和哲学家跟在车尾板后面走。

===Original===
“If you were feeling tired, or anything like that, Mister Honey,” said the woman, “you could climb up into the little cart, and nobody would say a word to you, for I can see that you are not used to travelling.”

“I am not indeed, ma’am,” he replied; “this is the first time I ever came on a journey, and if it wasn’t for Angus Óg I wouldn’t put a foot out of my own place forever.”

“Put Angus Óg out of your head, my dear,” she replied, “for what would the likes of you and me be saying to a god. He might put a curse on us would sink us into the ground or burn us up like a grip of straw. Be contented now, I’m saying, for if there is a woman in the world who knows all things I am that woman myself, and if you tell your trouble to me I’ll tell you the thing to do just as good as Angus himself, and better perhaps.”

“That is very interesting,” said the Philosopher. “What kind of things do you know best?”

“If you were to ask one of them two men walking beside the ass they’d tell you plenty of things they saw me do when they could do nothing themselves. When there wasn’t a road to take anywhere I showed them a road, and when there wasn’t a bit of food in the world I gave them food, and when they were bet to the last I put shillings in their hands, and that’s the reason they wanted to marry me.”

“Do you call that kind of thing wisdom?” said the Philosopher.

“Why wouldn’t I?” said she. “Isn’t it wisdom to go through the world without fear and not to be hungry in a hungry hour?”

“I suppose it is,” he replied, “but I never thought of it that way myself.”

“And what would you call wisdom?”

===Chinese===
「您要是走乏了，或是怎么的，蜜糖先生，」女人说，「可以爬到小车上去，没人会说您一个字的。我看得出来，您不惯出门。」

「实在不惯，太太，」他答道，「这是我头一回出门远行；要不是为了安格斯·奥格，我这辈子绝不把脚迈出自家门槛一步。」

「把安格斯·奥格忘了吧，亲爱的，」她答道，「咱们这号人跟一位神有什么可说的。他兴许一咒就把咱们咒进地底，或者像一把稻草似的把咱们烧成灰。听我的话，知足吧；这世上要是有个无所不知的女人，那就是我本人。您把您的难处告诉我，我告诉您该怎么办，保准跟安格斯本人说的一样好，兴许还好些。」

「这非常有意思，」哲学家说，「您最懂哪一类事情？」

「您去问驴子旁边那两个男人好了，他们能给您数出多少桩他们一筹莫展、而我把事情办成的事。没路可走的时候，我给他们指出路；世上断了粮的时候，我给他们弄到吃的；他们输到精光的时候，我往他们手里塞先令。他们就是为了这个才想娶我。」

「您管这个叫智慧？」哲学家说。

「怎么不叫？」她说，「走遍天下心不发慌，到了饿的时候不挨饿，这不是智慧是什么？」

「我想是吧，」他答道，「只是我自己从没这么想过。」

「那您管什么叫智慧？」

===Original===
“I couldn’t rightly say now,” he replied, “but I think it was not to mind about the world, and not to care whether you were hungry or not, and not to live in the world at all but only in your own head, for the world is a tyrannous place. You have to raise yourself above things instead of letting things raise themselves above you. We must not be slaves to each other, and we must not be slaves to our necessities either. That is the problem of existence. There is no dignity in life at all if hunger can shout ‘stop’ at every turn of the road and the day’s journey is measured by the distance between one sleep and the next sleep. Life is all slavery, and Nature is driving us with the whips of appetite and weariness; but when a slave rebels he ceases to be a slave, and when we are too hungry to live we can die and have our laugh. I believe that Nature is just as alive as we are, and that she is as much frightened of us as we are of her, and, mind you this, mankind has declared war against Nature and we will win. She does not understand yet that her geologic periods won’t do any longer, and that while she is pattering along the line of least resistance we are going to travel fast and far until we find her, and then, being a female, she is bound to give in when she is challenged.”

“It’s good talk,” said the woman, “but it’s foolishness. Women never give in unless they get what they want, and where’s the harm to them then? You have to live in the world, my dear, whether you like it or not, and, believe me now, that there isn’t any wisdom but to keep clear of the hunger, for if that gets near enough it will make a hare of you. Sure, listen to reason now like a good man. What is Nature at all but a word that learned men have made to talk about. There’s clay and gods and men, and they are good friends enough.”

===Chinese===
「我说不很准，」他答道，「但我想，智慧是不把世界放在心上，饿不饿全不在意；压根儿不住在世界里，只住在自己的头脑里——因为世界是个霸道的地方。你得把自己抬到事情之上，而不能让事情把你压在底下。我们既不能互为奴仆，也不能做自身必需品的奴仆。这就是存在的问题。倘若饿每到路口都能喊一声『停』，一天的旅程只能用一觉到下一觉的距离来量，人生便毫无尊严可言。生活全是奴役，自然正用食欲与疲倦的鞭子赶我们；可奴隶一反叛，就不再是奴隶；当我们饿得活不下去时，我们可以死去，笑上一场。我相信自然同我们一样是活的，她怕我们，正如我们怕她；记住我这句话：人类已经向自然宣战，我们必胜。她还不明白，她的地质纪年再也行不通了；她还在阻力最小的线上磨蹭，我们却要疾行远征，直到把她找出来；而她既是女的，一旦被挑战，就注定要认输。」

「话说得好，」女人说，「可惜是糊涂话。女人除非得到了她想要的，否则决不认输——那对她又有什么坏处呢？您反正得住在这个世界上，亲爱的，不管您乐意不乐意；相信我的话，世上没有什么智慧，比躲开饥饿更要紧，因为饥饿一旦凑得够近，能把您变成一只野兔。听劝吧，做个好汉子。什么自然不自然，不过是读书人造出来空谈的一个词儿。世上有泥土，有神，有人，他们本来是顶要好的朋友。」

===Original===
The sun had long since gone down, and the grey evening was bowing over the land, hiding the mountain peaks, and putting a shadow round the scattered bushes and the wide clumps of heather.

“I know a place up here where we can stop for the night,” said she, “and there’s a little shebeen round the bend of the road where we can get anything we want.”

At the word “whoh” the ass stopped and one of the men took the harness off him. When he was unyoked the man gave him two kicks: “Be off with you, you devil, and see if you can get anything to eat,” he roared. The ass trotted a few paces off and searched about until he found some grass. He ate this, and when he had eaten as much as he wanted he returned and lay down under a wall. He lay for a long time looking in the one direction, and at last he put his head down and went to sleep. While he was sleeping he kept one ear up and the other ear down for about twenty minutes, and then he put the first ear down and the other one up, and he kept on doing this all the night. If he had anything to lose you wouldn’t mind him setting up sentries, but he hadn’t a thing in the world except his skin and his bones, and no one would be bothered stealing them.

One of the men took a long bottle out of the cart and walked up the road with it. The other man lifted out a tin bucket which was punched all over with jagged holes. Then he took out some sods of turf and lumps of wood and he put these in the bucket, and in a few minutes he had a very nice fire lit. A pot of water was put on to boil, and the woman cut up a great lump of bacon which she put into the pot. She had eight eggs in a place in the cart, and a flat loaf of bread, and some cold boiled potatoes, and she spread her apron on the ground and arranged these things on it.

The other man came down the road again with his big bottle filled with porter, and he put this in a safe place. Then they emptied everything out of the cart and hoisted it over the little wall. They turned the cart on one side and pulled it near to the fire, and they all sat inside the cart and ate their supper. When supper was done they lit their pipes, and the woman lit a pipe also. The bottle of porter was brought forward, and they took drinks in turn out of the bottle, and smoked their pipes, and talked.

There was no moon that night, and no stars, so that just beyond the fire there was a thick darkness which one would not like to look at, it was so cold and empty. While talking they all kept their eyes fixed on the red fire, or watched the smoke from their pipes drifting and curling away against the blackness, and disappearing as suddenly as lightning.

===Chinese===
太阳早已落山，灰色的黄昏正俯身压向大地，藏起山峰，给散落的灌木和一丛丛宽阔的石楠都镶上一圈暗影。

「这儿上头有个地方可以过夜，」她说，「路拐过去有家私酒馆（shebeen），想要什么有什么。」

一声「吁」，驴子停了下来，一个男人动手给它卸套。卸完了，那人又踢了它两脚：「滚吧，你这魔鬼，自己找吃的去。」他吼道。驴子小跑几步走开，四处寻摸，寻到一片草。它吃了草，吃够了，便回来卧在一堵墙下。它朝着一个方向望了很久，终于垂下头睡着了。睡着的时候，它一只耳朵竖着、一只耳朵耷拉着，约莫这样过了二十分钟，然后换过来：先前竖着的耷拉下去，先前耷拉着的竖起来，一整夜就这么换个不停。它要是真有什么可丢的，倒也难怪它站岗放哨；可它在这世上除了一张皮一副骨头之外一无所有，谁肯费神去偷呢。

一个男人从车上取下一只长瓶，拎着它往路上头去了。另一个男人搬出一只浑身凿着豁口的铁皮桶。他又取出几块泥炭和几截木柴，放进桶里，不多几分钟就生起一炉极好的火。一锅水坐上去烧，女人切下一大块咸肉放进锅里。她在车里一处地方存着八个鸡蛋、一块扁面包、一些煮过的冷土豆，便把围裙铺在地上，把这些东西一样样摆好。

另一个男人拎着灌满黑啤酒的大瓶子从路上回来了，把它安放在稳妥的地方。随后他们把车上的东西卸空，连车抬过矮墙。他们把车侧翻过来，拖近火堆，众人坐进车斗里吃晚饭。晚饭吃完，他们点上烟斗，女人也点了一斗。黑啤酒的瓶子传了上来，他们轮着对瓶喝，抽着烟斗说话。

那夜没有月亮，也没有星星，火光之外便是浓黑一片，那样冷、那样空，叫人不忍去看。说话间，众人的眼睛都盯着红红的火，或者看烟斗里的烟在黑地里飘散、卷曲，像闪电一样倏然而没。

===Original===
“I wonder,” said the first man, “what it was gave you the idea of marrying this man instead of myself or my comrade, for we are young, hardy men, and he is getting old, God help him!”

“Aye, indeed,” said the second man; “he’s as grey as a badger, and there’s no flesh on his bones.”

“You have a right to ask that,” said she, “and I’ll tell you why I didn’t marry either of you. You are only a pair of tinkers going from one place to another, and not knowing anything at all of fine things; but himself was walking along the road looking for strange, high adventures, and it’s a man like that a woman would be wishing to marry if he was twice as old as he is. When did either of you go out in the daylight looking for a god and you not caring what might happen to you or where you went?”

“What I’m thinking,” said the second man, “is that if you leave the gods alone they’ll leave you alone. It’s no trouble to them to do whatever is right themselves, and what call would men like us have to go mixing or meddling with their high affairs?”

“I thought all along that you were a timid man,” said she, “and now I know it.” She turned again to the Philosopher—“Take off your boots, Mister Honey, the way you’ll rest easy, and I’ll be making down a soft bed for you in the cart.”

In order to take off his boots the Philosopher had to stand up, for in the cart they were too cramped for freedom. He moved backwards a space from the fire and took off his boots. He could see the woman stretching sacks and clothes inside the cart, and the two men smoking quietly and handing the big bottle from one to the other. Then in his stockinged feet he stepped a little farther from the fire, and, after another look, he turned and walked quietly away into the blackness. In a few minutes he heard a shout from behind him, and then a number of shouts and then these died away into a plaintive murmur of voices, and next he was alone in the greatest darkness he had ever known.

===Chinese===
「我一直纳闷，」第一个男人说，「是什么念头让您想起来嫁这个人，不嫁我或者我伙计——我们俩年轻力壮，他可上了年纪啦，上帝可怜他！」

「可不是嘛，」第二个男人说，「他灰得跟獾子似的，骨头缝里都没二两肉。」

「你们有权这么问，」她说，「我告诉你们我为什么不嫁你们俩任何一个。你们不过是两个流浪的补锅匠，从一处流浪到另一处，高雅的事一样不懂；他自己呢，走在路上寻找离奇崇高的奇遇——这样的男人，哪怕年纪再大一倍，女人也愿意嫁。你们俩哪一个，曾经在大白天出门去找一位神，连自己会怎么样、会走到哪儿去都不放在心上？」

「我一直在想，」第二个男人说，「你不去招惹神，神也不来招惹你。他们自家把该做的做对就是了，费不了什么事；像我们这号人，哪来的资格去掺和他们那些高高在上的事？」

「我早看出你是个胆小鬼，」她说，「现在更证明了。」她又转向哲学家——「脱了靴子吧，蜜糖先生，好歇得舒坦，我去车里给您铺个软和的铺。」

哲学家脱靴子得起身站着——坐在车斗里太局促，施展不开。他离火堆退开几步，脱下靴子。他看见女人在车里铺展麻袋和衣裳，两个男人静静地抽烟，把大瓶子递来递去。随后他穿着袜子，又悄悄离火堆远了一点，再望了一眼，便转身悄悄走进黑夜里去了。几分钟后，他听见身后一声呼喊，接着是好多声呼喊，再后来喊声低落成一片哀哀的人语，然后，他独自置身于生平所知最深沉的黑暗里。

===Original===
He put on his boots and walked onwards. He had no idea where the road lay, and every moment he stumbled into a patch of heather or prickly furze. The ground was very uneven with unexpected mounds and deep hollows: here and there were water-soaked, soggy places, and into these cold ruins he sank ankle deep. There was no longer an earth or a sky, but only a black void and a thin wind and a fierce silence which seemed to listen to him as he went. Out of that silence a thundering laugh might boom at an instant and stop again while he stood appalled in the blind vacancy.

The hill began to grow more steep and rocks were lying everywhere in his path. He could not see an inch in front, and so he went with his hands outstretched like a blind man who stumbles painfully along. After a time he was nearly worn out with cold and weariness, but he dared not sit down anywhere; the darkness was so intense that it frightened him, and the overwhelming, crafty silence frightened him also.

At last, and at a great distance, he saw a flickering, waving light, and he went towards this through drifts of heather, and over piled rocks and sodden bogland. When he came to the light he saw it was a torch of thick branches, the flame whereof blew hither and thither on the wind. The torch was fastened against a great cliff of granite by an iron band. At one side there was a dark opening in the rock, so he said: “I will go in there and sleep until the morning comes,” and he went in. At a very short distance the cleft turned again to the right, and here there was another torch fixed. When he turned this corner he stood for an instant in speechless astonishment, and then he covered his face and bowed down upon the ground.

===Chinese===
他穿上靴子，继续前行。他全然不知路在哪里，每一步都可能跌进一丛石楠或一片带刺的荆豆。地面坑洼难测，忽而土墩、忽而深洼；四处还有浸透水的烂泥塘，他一脚脚陷进这些冰冷的废墟里，没到脚踝。天地都不复

### XII / 第十二章

===Original===
Caitilin Ni Murrachu was sitting alone in the little cave behind Gort na Cloca Mora. Her companion had gone out as was his custom to walk in the sunny morning and to sound his pipe in desolate, green spaces whence, perhaps, the wanderer of his desire might hear the guiding sweetness. As she sat she was thinking. The last few days had awakened her body, and had also awakened her mind, for with the one awakening comes the other. The despondency which had touched her previously when tending her father’s cattle came to her again, but recognizably now. She knew the thing which the wind had whispered in the sloping field and for which she had no name—it was Happiness. Faintly she shadowed it forth, but yet she could not see it. It was only a pearl-pale wraith, almost formless, too tenuous to be touched by her hands, and too aloof to be spoken to. Pan had told her that he was the giver of happiness, but he had given her only unrest and fever and a longing which could not be satisfied. Again there was a want, and she could not formulate, or even realize it with any closeness. Her newborn Thought had promised everything, even as Pan, and it had given—she could not say that it had given her nothing or anything. Its limits were too quickly divinable. She had found the Tree of Knowledge, but about on every side a great wall soared blackly enclosing her in from the Tree of Life—a wall which her thought was unable to surmount even while instinct urged that it must topple before her advance; but instinct may not advance when thought has schooled it in the science of unbelief; and this wall will not be conquered until Thought and Instinct are wed, and the first son of that bridal will be called The Scaler of the Wall.

===Chinese===
卡蒂琳·尼·麦克穆拉赫独自坐在戈特纳克洛卡莫拉后头的小山洞里。她的同伴照习惯出门去了，在晴朗的早晨散步，在荒凉苍翠的空谷里吹响他的笛子——好教他盼望的那个游荡者，或许能听见那引路的甜美笛音。她坐着，想着心事。过去几日唤醒了她的身体，也唤醒了她的心智——一种觉醒总是伴着另一种降临。先前替父亲放牛时掠过她心头的那种沮丧，又回来了，只是如今她认得它了。她知道了风在斜坡田野里低语的那件东西，那件她一直叫不出名字的东西——那是幸福。她隐约描出它的轮廓，却仍旧看不见它。它只是一个珠灰色的幽灵，几乎无形，稀薄得她的手触不到，缥缈得无法同它说话。潘告诉过她，他是幸福的赐予者，可他给她的只有不宁、燥热，和一种无法餍足的渴望。如今又生出一种匮乏，她说不清它，甚至无法贴近地觉知它。她新生的思想许诺了一切，正如潘一样；而它给了她——她说不上它什么也没给，还是给了什么。它的界限一望便知。她找到了知识之树，可四周围耸起一堵黑色的高墙，把她隔在生命之树外面——她的思想翻不过这堵墙，尽管本能催促说：她一往前走，墙必倒塌；可当思想已经把不信的科学教给了本能，本能就再不能往前走了；这堵墙要到思想与本能成婚之日才会被攻破，那场婚礼的头生子，名字要叫做「越墙者」（The Scaler of the Wall）。

===Original===
So, after the quiet weariness of ignorance, the unquiet weariness of thought had fallen upon her. That travail of mind which, through countless generations, has throed to the birth of an ecstasy, the prophecy which humanity has sworn must be fulfilled, seeing through whatever mists and doubtings the vision of a gaiety wherein the innocence of the morning will not any longer be strange to our maturity.

While she was so thinking Pan returned, a little disheartened that he had found no person to listen to his pipings. He had been seated but a little time when suddenly, from without, a chorus of birds burst into joyous singing. Limpid and liquid cadenzas, mellow flutings, and the sweet treble of infancy met and danced and piped in the airy soundings. A round, soft tenderness of song rose and fell, broadened and soared, and then the high flight was snatched, eddied a moment, and was borne away to a more slender and wonderful loftiness, until, from afar, that thrilling song turned on the very apex of sweetness, dipped steeply and flashed its joyous return to the exultations of its mates below, rolling an ecstasy of song which for one moment gladdened the whole world and the sad people who moved thereon; then the singing ceased as suddenly as it began, a swift shadow darkened the passage, and Angus Óg came into the cave.

===Chinese===
于是，继无知那安静的倦怠之后，思想的烦乱倦怠又落到了她身上。那心智的产痛，历经无数世代，苦苦要分娩出一场心醉神迷；那人类起誓必应验的预言，穿过重重迷雾与疑虑，望见一幕欢愉的景象——在那欢愉里，清晨的无辜将不再与我们的成熟格格不入。

她正这样想着，潘回来了。他没有找到一个听他吹笛的人，有些垂头丧气。他坐下没多久，洞外忽然有一群鸟爆发出欢快的合唱。清亮流荡的顿音、柔润的长笛之音、婴孩般清甜的高音，在空明的音响里相逢、起舞、呜啭。一支圆润温柔的歌声扬起又落下，铺展又高翔，随即那高翔被一把攫住，回旋片刻，被带向更轻灵、更奇妙的凌云之处；直到从远处，那战栗的歌声在甜美的绝顶上倏然回转，陡然俯冲，欢快地闪回下方同伴的欢腾里，滚出一阵歌声的狂喜——那一刻，整个世界和世上忧愁的人们都被它照亮了；随后歌声戛然而止，如同它开始时一样突然。一道迅疾的影子暗了暗洞口，安格斯·奥格走进洞来。

===Original===
Caitilin sprang from her seat affrighted, and Pan also made a half movement towards rising, but instantly sank back again to his negligent, easy posture.

The god was slender and as swift as a wind. His hair swung about his face like golden blossoms. His eyes were mild and dancing and his lips smiled with quiet sweetness. About his head there flew perpetually a ring of singing birds, and when he spoke his voice came sweetly from a centre of sweetness.

“Health to you, daughter of Murrachu,” said he, and he sat down.

“I do not know you, sir,” the terrified girl whispered.

“I cannot be known until I make myself known,” he replied. “I am called Infinite Joy, O daughter of Murrachu, and I am called Love.”

===Chinese===
卡蒂琳惊得从座位上跳起来，潘也做了半个起身的动作，随即又沉回他那闲适随意的姿势里。

那位神祇身形修长，迅疾如风。他的头发像金色的花朵一样在脸旁摆动。他的眼睛温润而顾盼生辉，唇边噙着恬静的甜笑。他头顶永远飞旋着一环鸣啭的鸟儿，他一开口，声音便从一个甜美的中心甜甜地流出来。

「祝你安康，麦克穆拉赫的女儿。」他说着坐下了。

「我不认得您，先生。」吓坏了的姑娘低声说。

「我不让自己被认得，谁也认不得我，」他答道，「麦克穆拉赫的女儿，我名叫无穷之喜乐，我也名叫爱。」

===Original===
The girl gazed doubtfully from one to the other.

Pan looked up from his pipes.

“I also am called Love,” said he gently, “and I am called Joy.”

Angus Óg looked for the first time at Pan.

“Singer of the Vine,” said he, “I know your names—they are Desire and Fever and Lust and Death. Why have you come from your own place to spy upon my pastures and my quiet fields?”

===Chinese===
姑娘将信将疑地望望这个，又望望那个。

潘从笛子上抬起眼来。

「我也名叫爱，」他温和地说，「我也名叫喜乐。」

安格斯·奥格第一次正眼看了看潘。

「葡藤的歌者（Singer of the Vine），」他说，「我知道你的名字——它们是欲望、燥热、色欲和死亡。你为什么离开自己的地界，来窥探我的牧场和我安静的田地？」

===Original===
Pan replied mildly.

“The mortal gods move by the Immortal Will, and, therefore, I am here.”

“And I am here,” said Angus.

“Give me a sign,” said Pan, “that I must go.”

Angus Óg lifted his hand and from without there came again the triumphant music of the birds.

===Chinese===
潘温和地答道：

「可朽的神明们循不朽的意志而行，所以，我在这里。」

「我也在这里。」安格斯说。

「给我一个兆示，」潘说，「表明我必须走。」

安格斯·奥格举起手，洞外又传来鸟儿们得胜的音乐。

===Original===
“It is a sign,” said he, “the voice of Dana speaking in the air,” and, saying so, he made obeisance to the great mother.

Pan lifted his hand, and from afar there came the lowing of the cattle and the thin voices of the goats.

“It is a sign,” said he, “the voice of Demeter speaking from the earth,” and he also bowed deeply to the mother of the world.

Again Angus Óg lifted his hand, and in it there appeared a spear, bright and very terrible.

But Pan only said, “Can a spear divine the Eternal Will?” and Angus Óg put his weapon aside, and he said:

“The girl will choose between us, for the Divine Mood shines in the heart of man.”

===Chinese===
「这就是兆示，」他说，「达娜（Dana）在空中说话的声音。」说着，他向伟大的母亲俯身行礼。

潘举起手，远处传来牛的哞鸣和山羊细细的叫声。

「这就是兆示，」他说，「得墨忒耳（Demeter）从大地说话的声音。」他也向世界的母亲深深躬身。

安格斯·奥格又举起手，他手中出现了一支长矛，明亮而狰狞。

潘却只说：「长矛难道能占卜永恒的意志吗？」安格斯·奥格便把兵器放到一边，说道：

「这姑娘将在我们之间抉择，因为神圣的心境在人的心里发光。」

===Original===
Then Caitilin Ni Murrachu came forward and sat between the gods, but Pan stretched out his hand and drew her to him, so that she sat resting against his shoulder and his arm was about her body.

“We will speak the truth to this girl,” said Angus Óg.

“Can the gods speak otherwise?” said Pan, and he laughed with delight.

“It is the difference between us,” replied Angus Óg. “She will judge.”

===Chinese===
于是卡蒂琳·尼·麦克穆拉赫走上前，在两位神祇中间坐下；潘却伸出手把她拉到自己身边，让她倚着他的肩膀坐着，手臂环着她的身子。

「我们对这姑娘说实话吧。」安格斯·奥格说。

「神明还能说别的吗？」潘说着，欢畅地笑了。

「这正是你我之间的分别，」安格斯·奥格答道，「让她来裁断。」

===Original===
“Shepherd Girl,” said Pan, pressing her with his arm, “you will judge between us. Do you know what is the greatest thing in the world?—because it is of that you will have to judge.”

“I have heard,” the girl replied, “two things called the greatest things. You,” she continued to Pan, “said it was Hunger, and long ago my father said that Commonsense was the greatest thing in the world.”

“I have not told you,” said Angus Óg, “what I consider is the greatest thing in the world.”

“It is your right to speak,” said Pan.

“The greatest thing in the world,” said Angus Óg, “is the Divine Imagination.”

===Chinese===
「牧羊女，」潘用臂膀紧了紧她，「你来裁断我们之间的争执。你可知道世上最伟大的东西是什么？——因为你要裁断的正是这个。」

「我听说过，」姑娘答道，「有两样东西被称作最伟大的。你，」她转向潘，接着说，「说那是饥饿；很久以前，我父亲说，常识是世上最伟大的东西。」

「我还没有告诉你，」安格斯·奥格说，「我认为世上最伟大的东西是什么。」

「你有权发言。」潘说。

「世上最伟大的东西，」安格斯·奥格说，「是神圣的想象力（the Divine Imagination）。」

===Original===
“Now,” said Pan, “we know all the greatest things and we can talk of them.”

“The daughter of Murrachu,” continued Angus Óg, “has told us what you think and what her father thinks, but she has not told us what she thinks herself. Tell us, Caitilin Ni Murrachu, what you think is the greatest thing in the world.”

So Caitilin Ni Murrachu thought for a few moments and then replied timidly.

“I think that Happiness is the greatest thing in the world,” said she.

Hearing this they sat in silence for a little time, and then Angus Óg spoke again—

===Chinese===
「好了，」潘说，「所有的最伟大的东西我们都知道了，可以谈论它们了。」

「麦克穆拉赫的女儿，」安格斯·奥格接着说，「已经告诉了我们你怎么想、她父亲怎么想，可她还没告诉我们她自己怎么想。告诉我们，卡蒂琳·尼·麦克穆拉赫，你认为世上最伟大的东西是什么。」

于是卡蒂琳·尼·麦克穆拉赫想了几想，然后怯怯地答道：

「我认为，幸福是世上最伟大的东西。」她说。

听了这话，他们默然坐了片刻，随后安格斯·奥格又开口了——

===Original===
“The Divine Imagination may only be known through the thoughts of His creatures. A man has said Commonsense and a woman has said Happiness are the greatest things in the world. These things are male and female, for Commonsense is Thought and Happiness is Emotion, and until they embrace in Love the will of Immensity cannot be fruitful. For, behold, there has been no marriage of humanity since time began. Men have but coupled with their own shadows. The desire that sprang from their heads they pursued, and no man has yet known the love of a woman. And women have mated with the shadows of their own hearts, thinking fondly that the arms of men were about them. I saw my son dancing with an Idea, and I said to him, ‘With what do you dance, my son?’ and he replied, ‘I make merry with the wife of my affection,’ and truly she was shaped as a woman is shaped, but it was an Idea he danced with and not a woman. And presently he went away to his labours, and then his Idea arose and her humanity came upon her so that she was clothed with beauty and terror, and she went apart and danced with the servant of my son, and there was great joy of that dancing—for a person in the wrong place is an Idea and not a person. Man is Thought and woman is Intuition, and they have never mated. There is a gulf between them and it is called Fear, and what they fear is, that their strengths shall be taken from them and they may no longer be tyrants. The Eternal has made love blind, for it is not by science, but by intuition alone, that he may come to his beloved: but desire, which is science, has many eyes and sees so vastly that he passes his love in the press, saying there is no love, and he propagates miserably on his own delusions. The fingertips are guided by God, but the devil looks through the eyes of all creatures so that they may wander in the errors of reason and justify themselves of their wanderings. The desire of a man shall be Beauty, but he has fashioned a slave in his mind and called it Virtue. The desire of a woman shall be Wisdom, but she has formed a beast in her blood and called it Courage: but the real virtue is courage, and the real courage is liberty, and the real liberty is wisdom, and Wisdom is the son of Thought and Intuition; and his names also are Innocence and Adoration and Happiness.”

===Chinese===
「神圣的想象力，唯有透过他造物的思想才能被认识。一个男人说了常识，一个女人说了幸福，说它们是世上最伟大的东西。这两样东西，一男一女：因为常识是思想，幸福是情感；要等到它们在爱中相拥，浩瀚的意志才能结出果实。看哪，自时间之始，人类就未曾有过真正的婚配。男人不过与他们自己的影子交合。他们追逐从自己头脑里生出的欲望，还没有一个男人认识过女人的爱。女人们也与她们自己心头的影子相配，痴痴以为男人的手臂正搂着她们。我看见我的儿子与一个观念共舞，我问他：『我的儿，你与什么共舞？』他答道：『我与我的爱妻同乐。』她的身段果真像女人的身段，可与他共舞的是一个观念，不是女人。不久他出门去做他的工，他的观念便站起来，人性临到她身上，她披上了美与可畏，走开去与我儿子的仆人共舞，那场舞蹈有莫大的欢喜——因为一个人放错了地方，就只是一个观念，不再是人。男人是思想，女人是直觉，他们从未真正相配。他们之间隔着一道鸿沟，名叫恐惧；他们所怕的，是自己的力量被夺去，再不能做暴君。永恒者把爱造成盲的，因为唯有凭直觉、不凭科学，他才能走到挚爱跟前；而欲望，它就是科学，它有无数只眼睛，看得那样辽阔，以致在人群里与自己的爱擦肩而过，口里还说世上没有爱，然后在自家的错觉里凄惨地繁衍。指尖由上帝引路，魔鬼却从一切造物的眼睛里向外窥望，好让他们在理性的错误里流浪，并为自己的流浪辩护。男人的欲望本应是美，他却在自己头脑里造出一个奴隶，称它为德性。女人的欲望本应是智慧，她却在自己的血液里养出一头野兽，称它为勇气：可真正的德性是勇气，真正的勇气是自由，真正的自由是智慧；智慧是思想与直觉之子，他的名字也是无辜、崇敬与幸福。」

===Original===
When Angus Óg had said these words he ceased, and for a time there was silence in the little cave. Caitilin had covered her face with her hands and would not look at him, but Pan drew the girl closer to his side and peered sideways, laughing at Angus.

“Has the time yet come for the girl to judge between us?” said he.

“Daughter of Murrachu,” said Angus Óg, “will you come away with me from this place?”

Caitilin then looked at the god in great distress.

“I do not know what to do,” said she. “Why do you both want me? I have given myself to Pan, and his arms are about me.”

===Chinese===
安格斯·奥格说完这些话就停住了，小山洞里一时寂静。卡蒂琳双手掩面，不肯看他；潘却把姑娘往身边搂得更紧，斜着眼笑望安格斯。

「姑娘裁断我们的时刻到了吗？」他说。

「麦克穆拉赫的女儿，」安格斯·奥格说，「你肯跟我离开这个地方吗？」

卡蒂琳十分惶惑地望着那位神。

「我不知道该怎么办，」她说，「你们为什么都要我？我已经把自己给了潘，他的手臂正搂着我。」

===Original===
“I want you,” said Angus Óg, “because the world has forgotten me. In all my nation there is no remembrance of me. I, wandering on the hills of my country, am lonely indeed. I am the desolate god forbidden to utter my happy laughter. I hide the silver of my speech and the gold of my merriment. I live in the holes of the rocks and the dark caves of the sea. I weep in the morning because I may not laugh, and in the evening I go abroad and am not happy. Where I have kissed a bird has flown; where I have trod a flower has sprung. But Thought has snared my birds in his nets and sold them in the marketplaces. Who will deliver me from Thought, from the base holiness of Intellect, the maker of chains and traps? Who will save me from the holy impurity of Emotion, whose daughters are Envy and Jealousy and Hatred, who plucks my flowers to ornament her lusts and my little leaves to shrivel on the breasts of infamy? Lo, I am sealed in the caves of nonentity until the head and the heart shall come together in fruitfulness, until Thought has wept for Love, and Emotion has purified herself to meet her lover. Tir-na-nÓg is the heart of a man and the head of a woman. Widely they are separated. Self-centred they stand, and between them the seas of space are flooding desolately. No voice can shout across those shores. No eye can bridge them, nor any desire bring them together until the blind god shall find them on the wavering stream—not as an arrow searches straightly from a bow, but gently, imperceptibly as a feather on the wind reaches the ground on a hundred starts; not with the compass and the chart, but by the breath of the Almighty which blows from all quarters without care and without ceasing. Night and day it urges from the outside to the inside. It gathers ever to the centre. From the far without to the deep within, trembling from the body to the soul until the head of a woman and the heart of a man are filled with the Divine Imagination. Hymen, Hymenaea! I sing to the ears that are stopped, the eyes that are sealed, and the minds that do not labour. Sweetly I sing on the hillside. The blind shall look within and not without; the deaf shall hearken to the murmur of their own veins, and be enchanted with the wisdom of sweetness; the thoughtless shall think without effort as the lightning flashes, that the hand of Innocence may reach to the stars, that the feet of Adoration may dance to the Father of Joy, and the laugh of Happiness be answered by the Voice of Benediction.”

===Chinese===
「我要你，」安格斯·奥格说，「因为这世界已经把我忘了。在我的族人里，没有人再记得我。我，在我祖国的山丘间游荡，实在孤独。我是那凄凉的神，被禁止发出幸福的笑声。我藏起我言语的银子和我欢乐的金子。我住在岩穴里，住在大海幽暗的洞窟里。清晨我哭泣，因为不许我笑；傍晚我出来游走，并不快乐。我吻过的地方，有鸟儿飞起；我踏过的地方，有花朵迸出。可思想用他的网捉走了我的鸟，拿到市集上去卖。谁能救我脱离思想，脱离理智那卑贱的神圣——那锁链与陷阱的制造者？谁能救我脱离情感那神圣的不洁——她的女儿们是嫉妒、猜忌与憎恨，她摘我的花去装点她的淫欲，摘我的小叶去在耻辱的胸前枯萎？看哪，我被封在虚无的洞窟里，直到头脑与心灵在丰饶中相逢，直到思想为爱哭泣，直到情感洁净了自己去迎她的爱人。提尔纳诺格（Tir-na-nÓg）是男人的心、女人的头。它们相隔万里。它们各自以自己为中心矗立着，中间是太空的海洋凄凉地泛滥。没有声音能喊过那些海岸。没有眼睛能跨越它们，也没有欲望能把它们聚拢——直到盲神在摇荡的水流上找到它们：不像箭从弓上笔直地搜寻，而像风中的一片羽毛，凭着上百次的起落，轻轻地、无声无息地落到地面；不凭罗盘与海图，只凭全能者的呼吸——那气息从四方吹来，无牵无挂，无休无止。它日夜从外催向内。它永远向中心汇聚。从遥远的体外到幽深的内里，从身体颤到灵魂，直到女人的头与男人的心都盛满了神圣的想象力。许门啊，许门埃（Hymen, Hymenaea）！我对堵塞的耳朵唱，对封闭的眼睛唱，对不肯用功的心智唱。我在山坡上甜甜地唱。盲者将向内看，不再向外看；聋者将谛听自己血脉的低语，为甜美的智慧所迷醉；不思想的人将不费力气地思想，如电光一闪，好教无辜的手伸向星辰，教崇敬的脚向喜乐之父起舞，教幸福的笑声得到祝福之声的回答。」

===Original===
Thus Angus Óg sang in the cave, and ere he had ceased Caitilin Ni Murrachu withdrew herself from the arms of her desires. But so strong was the hold of Pan upon her that when she was free her body bore the marks of his grip, and many days passed away before these marks faded.

Then Pan arose in silence, taking his double reed in his hand, and the girl wept, beseeching him to stay to be her brother and the brother of her beloved, but Pan smiled and said:

“Your beloved is my father and my son. He is yesterday and tomorrow. He is the nether and the upper millstone, and I am crushed between until I kneel again before the throne from whence I came,” and, saying so, he embraced Angus Óg most tenderly and went his way to the quiet fields, and across the slopes of the mountains, and beyond the blue distances of space.

And in a little time Caitilin Ni Murrachu went with her companion across the brow of the hill, and she did not go with him because she had understood his words, nor because he was naked and unashamed, but only because his need of her was very great, and, therefore, she loved him, and stayed his feet in the way, and was concerned lest he should stumble.

===Chinese===
安格斯·奥格就这样在洞中歌唱。他还没唱完，卡蒂琳·尼·麦克穆拉赫已经从她欲望的手臂里抽身出来了。可潘搂她搂得那样紧，等她脱开身时，她身上还留着他的指痕，过了许多天，这些痕迹才褪尽。

随后潘默然起身，手里拿着他的双管笛。姑娘哭了，恳求他留下来，做她的兄长，做她所爱之人的兄长。潘却笑着说：

「你所爱的，是我的父亲，也是我的儿子。他是昨天，又是明天。他是下磨扇与上磨扇，我被碾磨在中间，直到我再度跪拜在我所来自的那宝座之前。」说着，他极尽温柔地拥抱了安格斯·奥格，上路去了——走向安静的田野，走过山峦的坡面，走向空间蓝色的远方之外。

不多时，卡蒂琳·尼·麦克穆拉赫便与她的同伴翻过了山脊。她跟他走，不是因为她懂了他的话，也不是因为他赤身露体而无羞，只因为他对她的需要极大；因此她爱他，在路上扶住他的脚，唯恐他跌倒。

### XIII / 第十三章

===Original===
Which is, the Earth or the creatures that move upon it, the more important? This is a question prompted solely by intellectual arrogance, for in life there is no greater and no less. The thing that *is* has justified its own importance by mere existence, for that is the great and equal achievement. If life were arranged for us from without such a question of supremacy would assume importance, but life is always from within, and is modified or extended by our own appetites, aspirations, and central activities. From without we get pollen and the refreshment of space and quietude—it is sufficient. We might ask, is the Earth anything more than an extension of our human consciousness, or are we, moving creatures, only projections of the Earth’s antennae? But these matters have no value save as a field wherein Thought, like a wise lamb, may frolic merrily. And all would be very well if Thought would but continue to frolic, instead of setting up first as locum tenens for Intuition and sticking to the job, and afterwards as the counsel and critic of Omnipotence. Everything has two names, and everything is twofold. The name of male Thought as it faces the world is Philosophy, but the name it bears in Tir-na-nÓg is Delusion. Female Thought is called Socialism on earth, but in Eternity it is known as Illusion; and this is so because there has been no matrimony of minds, but only an hermaphroditic propagation of automatic ideas, which in their due rotation assume dominance and reign severely. To the world this system of thought, because it is consecutive, is known as Logic, but Eternity has written it down in the Book of Errors as Mechanism: for life may not be consecutive, but explosive and variable, else it is a shackled and timorous slave.

===Chinese===
究竟是大地重要，还是在大地上活动的生灵重要？这个问题全然出自理智的傲慢，因为生命之中本无大小高低之分。凡是*存在*的东西，仅凭存在便已证明了自己的重要——那才是伟大而平等的成就。倘若生活是由外部替我们安排的，这种争先后的问题或许要紧；可生命永远发自内部，并且只被我们自己的食欲、向往与中心活动所修饰、所延展。从外部，我们得到花粉，得到空间与宁静的滋养——这就够了。我们或许要问：大地会不会只是人类意识的延伸？抑或我们这些会动的生灵，不过是大地触角的投影？但这些题目毫无价值，只堪作一片田野，供思想像一只聪明的小羊在里面欢快地蹦跳。要是思想肯一直蹦跳下去，倒也万事大吉；可惜它先僭居了直觉的临时代理，还赖着不走，随后又当起了全能者的顾问与批评家。万事万物都有两个名字，万事万物都是两重的。男性的思想面对世界时的名字叫哲学，它在提尔纳诺格的名字却叫迷妄。女性的思想在地上被称作社会主义，在永恒里却被称为幻妄；之所以如此，是因为心灵之间从未有过婚配，有的只是自动观念的两性同体式繁衍——这些观念按着各自的轮次凌驾在上，严厉地君临一切。在世人看来，这套思想体系因为前后连贯，被称作逻辑；永恒却已在《错误之书》里把它记作机械论：因为生命可以不是连贯的，而必须爆裂、多变，否则它就只是一个戴着镣铐、战战兢兢的奴隶。

===Original===
One of the great troubles of life is that Reason has taken charge of the administration of Justice, and by mere identification it has achieved the crown and sceptre of its master. But the imperceptible usurpation was recorded, and discriminating minds understand the chasm which still divides the pretender Law from the exiled King. In a like manner, and with feigned humility, the Cold Demon advanced to serve Religion, and by guile and violence usurped her throne; but the pure in heart still fly from the spectre Theology to dance in ecstasy before the starry and eternal goddess. Statecraft, also, that tender Shepherd of the Flocks, has been despoiled of his crook and bell, and wanders in unknown desolation while, beneath the banner of Politics, Reason sits howling over an intellectual chaos.

===Chinese===
人生的一大祸患，在于理性篡掌了司法的行政，仅凭一次冒认，它就拿到了主人的冠冕与权杖。可那场不易察觉的篡位早被记录在案，目光敏锐的心智都明白：僭称的「法律」与遭放逐的「国王」之间，至今横着一道鸿沟。同样的手法，又伴着假意的谦卑，「寒冷的魔鬼」（the Cold Demon）趋前伺奉宗教，靠欺诈与强暴篡了她的王位；可心地纯洁的人依旧逃离「神学」这个幽灵，在群星闪烁的永恒女神面前狂喜起舞。还有治国之术，那位羊群的温良牧人，也被夺走了曲柄杖与铃铛，在无人知晓的荒凉里流浪；而在「政治」的旗帜底下，理性正对着一堆理智的乱墟嚎叫。

===Original===
Justice is the maintaining of equilibrium. The blood of Cain must cry, not from the lips of the Avenger, but from the aggrieved Earth herself who demands that atonement shall be made for a disturbance of her consciousness. All justice is, therefore, readjustment. A thwarted consciousness has every right to clamour for assistance, but not for punishment. This latter can only be sought by timorous and egotistic Intellect, which sees the Earth from which it has emerged and into which it must return again in its own despite, and so, being self-centred and envious and a renegade from life, Reason is more cruelly unjust, and more timorous than any other manifestation of the divinely erratic energy—erratic, because, as has been said, “the crooked roads are the roads of genius.” Nature grants to all her creatures an unrestricted liberty, quickened by competitive appetite, to succeed or to fail; save only to Reason, her Demon of Order, which can do neither, and whose wings she has clipped for some reason with which I am not yet acquainted. It may be that an unrestricted mentality would endanger her own intuitive perceptions by shackling all her other organs of perception, or annoy her by vexatious efforts at creative rivalry.

===Chinese===
公义就是维持平衡。该隐的血必得哀告——不是从复仇者口中，而是从受害的大地自己口中，她要求为自己意识的扰动得到补偿。所以一切公义都是重新调平。受挫的意识完全有权呼求援助，却无权呼求惩罚。惩罚只能是那怯懦而自私的理智去寻求的东西：理智看见自己从中涌出、又必得违背本愿返回的大地，于是它自我中心、满怀嫉妒、叛离了生命，比神圣而任性的能量任何别的显现都更残酷地不公，也更怯懦——说是任性，因为正如那句话所说：「曲折之路，才是天才之路。」自然赋予她的一切造物一种不受约束的自由，由竞争的食欲催动着，去成功或去失败；唯独对理智，她的秩序之魔，她不肯给：理智既不能成功也不能失败，它的翅膀还被她剪去了，缘由何在，我还不得而知。也许，一种不受约束的心智会锁住她其他一切知觉的器官，从而危及她自己的直觉；也许，它还会以恼人的创造性竞争来搅扰她。

===Original===
It will, therefore, be understood that when the Leprecauns of Gort na Cloca Mora acted in the manner about to be recorded, they were not prompted by any lewd passion for revenge, but were merely striving to reconstruct a rhythm which was their very existence, and which must have been of direct importance to the Earth. Revenge is the vilest passion known to life. It has made Law possible, and by doing so it gave to Intellect the first grip at that universal dominion which is its ambition. A Leprecaun is of more value to the Earth than is a Prime Minister or a stockbroker, because a Leprecaun dances and makes merry, while a Prime Minister knows nothing of these natural virtues—consequently, an injury done to a Leprecaun afflicts the Earth with misery, and justice is, for these reasons, an imperative and momentous necessity.

===Chinese===
因此可以明白：戈特纳克洛卡莫拉的矮妖们做出下面将要记下的那些行径时，并不是被什么下贱的复仇欲所驱使，而只是竭力想重建一种节奏——那节奏就是他们的存在本身，而且必定与大地有直接的利害。复仇是生命所知的最卑贱的情欲。它使「法律」成为可能，并由此给了理智第一把抓手，去攫取它觊觎的普世统治。对大地而言，一个矮妖比一位首相或一个股票经纪人更有价值，因为矮妖会跳舞、会作乐，而首相对这些天然的美德一无所知——因此，加于矮妖的伤害必使大地陷于悲苦；正因如此，公义才是紧迫而重大的必需。

===Original===
A community of Leprecauns without a crock of gold is a blighted and merriless community, and they are certainly justified in seeking sympathy and assistance for the recovery of so essential a treasure. But the steps whereby the Leprecauns of Gort na Cloca Mora sought to regain their property must forever brand their memory with a certain odium. It should be remembered in their favour that they were cunningly and cruelly encompassed. Not only was their gold stolen, but it was buried in such a position as placed it under the protection of their own communal honour, and the household of their enemy was secured against their active and righteous malice, because the Thin Woman of Inis Magrath belonged to the most powerful Shee of Ireland. It is in circumstances such as these that dangerous alliances are made, and, for the first time in history, the elemental beings invoked bourgeois assistance.

They were loath to do it, and justice must record the fact. They were angry when they did it, and anger is both mental and intuitive blindness. It is not the beneficent blindness which prevents one from seeing without, but it is that desperate darkness which cloaks the within, and hides the heart and the brain from each other’s husbandry and wifely recognition. But even those mitigating circumstances cannot justify the course they adopted, and the wider idea must be sought for, that out of evil good must ultimately come, or else evil is vitiated beyond even the redemption of usage. When they were able to realize of what they had been guilty, they were very sorry indeed, and endeavoured to publish their repentance in many ways; but, lacking atonement, repentance is only a postmortem virtue which is good for nothing but burial.

===Chinese===
一个没有金罐的矮妖社群，是一个凋敝而没有欢乐的社群；他们为找回这样一件要紧的宝物而去寻求同情与援助，当然是正当的。但戈特纳克洛卡莫拉的矮妖们为夺回财产所走的那些步骤，将永远给他们的名声烙上某种污点。为他们说句公道话：他们是被人狡诈而残忍地设了局。他们的金子不但被偷走，还被埋在这样一个位置，恰好置于他们自己族群荣誉的护卫之下；他们仇家的宅子也得以免受他们理直气壮的怨毒，因为伊尼斯马格拉的瘦女人属于爱尔兰最有势力的一支希神族。正是在这样的情势里，危险的联盟缔结起来——有史以来头一遭，元素生灵（the elemental beings）竟去求起了市侩的帮助。

他们不情愿这样做，公义必须记下这一事实。他们做这事时正在气头上，而愤怒既是心智的盲，也是直觉的盲。它不是那种使人看不见外部的有益的盲，而是那种遮蔽内部的绝望的黑暗，它叫心与脑彼此看不见对方的持家与相认。可纵有这些减轻罪责的情节，也不能为他们选择的行径开脱；还得去寻求一个更宽的意念：恶的尽头终须结出善来，否则恶就败坏得连习常也救赎不得。待他们能够意识到自己犯了什么，他们确实非常懊悔，还多方设法公布自己的悔恨；可是没有补赎，悔恨只是一种死后的美德，除了下葬之外一无所用。

===Original===
When the Leprecauns of Gort na Cloca Mora found they were unable to regain their crock of gold by any means they laid an anonymous information at the nearest Police Station showing that two dead bodies would be found under the hearthstone in the hut of Coille Doraca, and the inference to be drawn from their crafty missive was that these bodies had been murdered by the Philosopher for reasons very discreditable to him.

The Philosopher had been scarcely more than three hours on his journey to Angus Óg when four policemen approached the little house from as many different directions, and without any trouble they effected an entrance. The Thin Woman of Inis Magrath and the two children heard from afar their badly muffled advance, and on discovering the character of their visitors they concealed themselves among the thickly clustering trees. Shortly after the men had entered the hut loud and sustained noises began to issue therefrom, and in about twenty minutes the invaders emerged again bearing the bodies of the Grey Woman of Dun Gortin and her husband. They wrenched the door off its hinges, and, placing the bodies on the door, proceeded at a rapid pace through the trees and disappeared in a short time. When they had departed the Thin Woman and the children returned to their home and over the yawning hearth the Thin Woman pronounced a long and fervid malediction wherein policemen were exhibited naked before the blushes of Eternity …

With your goodwill let us now return to the Philosopher.

===Chinese===
当戈特纳克洛卡莫拉的矮妖们发觉无论用什么办法也夺不回金罐时，他们向最近的警察局递了一封匿名举报，说在科拉多拉卡小屋的炉灶石底下将找到两具尸首；从他们那封刁滑的信函里可以推出的暗示是：这两个人是哲学家谋杀的，而杀人的缘由叫他极不体面。

哲学家动身去安格斯·奥格那里还不到三个钟头，四名警察便从四个方向朝小屋摸来，毫不费力就进了屋。伊尼斯马格拉的瘦女人和两个孩子远远听见了他们掩得很拙劣的脚步声，一辨明来者的身份，便藏进密密丛丛的树里。那几个人进屋不久，里面开始传出响亮而持续的声音；约莫二十分钟后，闯入者重新现身，抬着邓戈尔坦的灰衣女人和她丈夫的尸首。他们卸下门板，把尸首放在门上，抬着快步穿过树丛，不多时便不见了。他们走后，瘦女人和孩子们回到家里，瘦女人对着敞开的炉膛，发表了一通冗长而炽烈的咒詈，把警察们赤条条地陈列在永恒的羞红之前……

承诸位见谅，现在让我们回到哲学家那里。

===Original===
Following his interview with Angus Óg the Philosopher received the blessing of the god and returned on his homeward journey. When he left the cave he had no knowledge where he was nor whether he should turn to the right hand or to the left. This alone was his guiding idea, that as he had come up the mountain on his first journey his homegoing must, by mere opposition, be down the mountain, and, accordingly, he set his face downhill and trod lustily forward. He had stamped up the hill with vigour, he strode down it in ecstasy. He tossed his voice on every wind that went by. From the wells of forgetfulness he regained the shining words and gay melodies which his childhood had delighted in, and these he sang loudly and unceasingly as he marched. The sun had not yet risen but, far away, a quiet brightness was creeping over the sky. The daylight, however, was near the full, one slender veil only remaining of the shadows, and a calm, unmoving quietude brooded from the grey sky to the whispering earth. The birds had begun to bestir themselves but not to sing. Now and again a solitary wing feathered the chill air; but for the most part the birds huddled closer in the swinging nests, or under the bracken, or in the tufty grass. Here a faint twitter was heard and ceased. A little farther a drowsy voice called “cheep-cheep” and turned again to the warmth of its wing. The very grasshoppers were silent. The creatures who range in the nighttime had returned to their cells and were setting their households in order, and those who belonged to the day hugged their comfort for but one minute longer. Then the first level beam stepped like a mild angel to the mountain top. The slender radiance brightened and grew strong. The grey veil faded away. The birds leaped from their nests. The grasshoppers awakened and were busy at a stroke. Voice called to voice without ceasing, and, momently, a song thrilled for a few wide seconds. But for the most part it was chatter-chatter they went as they soared and plunged and swept, each bird eager for its breakfast.

The Philosopher thrust his hand into his wallet and found there the last broken remnants of his cake, and the instant his hand touched the food he was seized by a hunger so furious that he sat down where he stopped and prepared to eat.

The place where he sat was a raised bank under a hedge, and this place directly fronted a clumsy wooden gate leading into a great field. When the Philosopher had seated himself he raised his eyes and saw through the gate a small company approaching. There were four men and three women, and each of them carried a metal pail. The Philosopher with a sigh returned the cake to his wallet, saying:

“All men are brothers, and it may be that these people are as hungry as I am.”

===Chinese===
与安格斯·奥格会面之后，哲学家领受了神的祝福，踏上了归家的路程。走出山洞时，他既不知道自己身在何处，也不知道该向右还是向左。他唯一的向导之念是：来时是上山，那么回家就凭相反的方向，必是下山。于是他把脸朝向山下，起劲地走去。上山路他踏得雄健，下山路他走得心醉。他把嗓音抛给每一阵过路的风。他从遗忘之井里重新拾回了童年爱过的闪亮的字句和快活的调子，一路走着，放声唱个不停。太阳还没有升起，但远处，一片安静的亮光正悄悄爬上天空。天色却已近大亮，夜影只剩薄薄一层轻纱，一片沉静不动的安宁，从灰色的天空笼到低语的大地。鸟儿已开始蠢动，却还不曾歌唱。不时有一只孤鸟振翅划过寒气；但多半的鸟儿还在摇曳的巢里、在蕨丛下、在丛生的草窠里挤得更紧。这边一声细弱的啁啾，响起又止。稍远处一个惺忪的声音叫了声「唧唧」，又缩回翅膀的暖意里。连蚱蜢也缄默着。夜里巡游的生灵已经回到巢穴，正收拾各自的门庭；属于白昼的那些，则把安逸再搂紧一分钟。随后，第一道平直的光束像一位温柔的天使，踏上山顶。那纤细的光辉亮起来，渐渐强了。灰纱褪尽了。鸟儿从巢里一跃而起。蚱蜢醒来，霎时忙碌。叫声唤着叫声，没有停歇；不时有一支歌颤响几个开阔的瞬间。但多半还是叽叽喳喳——它们腾起、俯冲、掠过，只只都急着赶自己的早饭。

哲学家把手伸进褡裢，摸到饼最后的一点碎块；手一触到食物，一阵狂烈的饥饿便攫住了他，他当场坐下，准备开吃。

他坐的地方是树篱下一道高起的土坡，正对着一扇通向大片田野的笨重木门。哲学家坐定后，抬眼透过大门望见一小队人走近了。四男三女，每人都提着一只铁皮桶。哲学家叹了口气，把饼放回褡裢，说道：

「四海之内皆兄弟，说不定这些人跟我一样饿呢。」

===Original===
In a short time the strangers came near. The foremost of them was a huge man who was bearded to the eyelids and who moved like a strong wind. He opened the gate by removing a piece of wood wherewith it was jammed, and he and his companions passed through, whereupon he closed the gate and secured it. To this man, as being the eldest, the Philosopher approached.

“I am about to breakfast,” said he, “and if you are hungry perhaps you would like to eat with me.”

“Why not,” said the man, “for the person who would refuse a kind invitation is a dog. These are my three sons and three of my daughters, and we are all thankful to you.”

Saying this he sat down on the bank and his companions, placing their pails behind them, did likewise. The Philosopher divided his cake into eight pieces and gave one to each person.

“I am sorry it is so little,” said he.

“A gift,” said the bearded man, “is never little,” and he courteously ate his piece in three bites although he could have easily eaten it in one, and his children also made much of their pieces.

“That was a good, satisfying cake,” said he when he had finished; “it was well baked and well shared, but,” he continued, “I am in a difficulty and maybe you could advise me what to do, sir?”

“What might be your trouble?” said the Philosopher.

“It is this,” said the man. “Every morning when we go out to milk the cows the mother of my clann gives to each of us a parcel of food so that we need not be any hungrier than we like; but now we have had a good breakfast with you, what shall we do with the food that we brought with us? The woman of the house would not be pleased if we carried it back to her, and if we threw food away it would be a sin. If it was not disrespectful to your breakfast the boys and girls here might be able to get rid of it by eating it, for, as you know, young people can always eat a bit more, no matter how much they have already eaten.”

“It would surely be better to eat it than to waste it,” said the Philosopher wistfully.

The young people produced large parcels of food from their pockets and opened them, and the bearded man said, “I have a little one myself also, and it would not be wasted if you were kind enough to help me to eat it,” and he pulled out his parcel, which was twice as big as any of the others.

He opened the parcel and handed the larger part of its contents to the Philosopher; he then plunged a tin vessel into one of the milk pails and set this also by the Philosopher, and, instantly, they all began to eat with furious appetite.

===Chinese===
不多时，那些陌生人走近了。走在最前的是个大个子，胡子一直长到眼皮边，走动起来像一阵劲风。他抽掉抵住门的木条，推开大门，和同伴们走了进去，随后又把门关好拴牢。哲学家朝这人走去，因为他是长者。

「我正要用早饭，」他说，「你们要是饿了，也许愿意跟我一起吃。」

「有什么不愿意的，」那人说，「拒绝好意邀请的人是条狗。这是我的三个儿子、三个女儿，我们都很感激您。」

说着，他在土坡上坐下，同伴们把铁皮桶放在身后，也一一坐下了。哲学家把饼分成八份，每人分了一份。

「就这么一点，很抱歉。」他说。

「礼物，」大胡子说，「永远不嫌小。」他斯斯文文地分三口吃完了自己那份——其实他一口就能吞下；他的儿女们也都把各自的一份吃得很仔细。

「这饼又好又顶饱，」他吃完说道，「烙得好，分得也好。不过，」他接着说，「我正有个难处，先生，兴许您能给我出个主意？」

「你有什么难处？」哲学家说。

「是这样，」那人说，「每天早晨我们出去挤奶，孩子们他们的娘都给每人包一份吃食，好让我们想不饿就不饿。可如今跟您吃了顿好早饭，随身带的这些吃食可怎么处置？带回去还给家里的主妇，她要不高兴的；把吃食扔了，又是罪过。要是这不至于怠慢了您这顿早饭，这几个小子丫头也许能把它们吃掉——您知道的，年轻人不管已经吃了多少，总能再添一口。」

「吃了总比糟蹋了好。」哲学家幽幽地说。

年轻人们从口袋里掏出大包大包的食物，打了开来。大胡子说：「我自己也有一小份，您若肯帮我把它吃了，也不算浪费。」他掏出他那份——比谁的都大一倍。

他打开包，把大的一半递给哲学家；又把一只铁皮罐伸进一只奶桶里舀满，也搁在哲学家手边。霎时间，众人都风卷残云地吃了起来。

===Original===
When the meal was finished the Philosopher filled his tobacco pipe and the bearded man and his three sons did likewise.

“Sir,” said the bearded man, “I would be glad to know why you are travelling abroad so early in the morning, for, at this hour, no one stirs but the sun and the birds and the folk who, like ourselves, follow the cattle?”

“I will tell you that gladly,” said the Philosopher, “if you will tell me your name.”

“My name,” said the bearded man, “is Mac Cúl.”

“Last night,” said the Philosopher, “when I came from the house of Angus Óg in the Caves of the Sleepers of Erinn I was bidden say to a man named Mac Cúl—that the horses had trampled in their sleep and the sleepers had turned on their sides.”

“Sir,” said the bearded man, “your words thrill in my heart like music, but my head does not understand them.”

“I have learned,” said the Philosopher, “that the head does not hear anything until the heart has listened, and that what the heart knows today the head will understand tomorrow.”

“All the birds of the world are singing in my soul,” said the bearded man, “and I bless you because you have filled me with hope and pride.”

So the Philosopher shook him by the hand, and he shook the hands of his sons and daughters who bowed before him at the mild command of their father, and when he had gone a little way he looked around again and he saw that group of people standing where he had left them, and the bearded man was embracing his children on the high road.

===Chinese===
饭罢，哲学家装上烟斗，大胡子和他的三个儿子也各装了一斗。

「先生，」大胡子说，「我很想请教，您怎么这么一大清早就在外头赶路？这个时辰，除了太阳、鸟儿，还有我们这些跟牛打交道的人，没有谁会动身。」

「我乐意奉告，」哲学家说，「只要您肯告诉我您的名字。」

「我的名字，」大胡子说，「叫麦克·库尔（Mac Cúl）。」

「昨夜，」哲学家说，「我从爱林沉睡者之洞（the Caves of the Sleepers of Erinn）安格斯·奥格的居所回来时，受托向一位名叫麦克·库尔的人传话——马群在睡梦里踏了蹄，沉睡者们翻了身。」

「先生，」大胡子说，「您的话像音乐一样在我心里震颤，可我的头脑听不懂它们。」

「我已学到，」哲学家说，「心没有听过的，头脑什么也听不见；心今天知道的，头脑明天就会明白。」

「全世界的鸟都在我灵魂里歌唱，」大胡子说，「我祝福您，因为您使我充满了希望和骄傲。」

于是哲学家同他握手，又同他的儿女们握手——他们在父亲温和的吩咐下向哲学家躬身行礼。走出一段路，他回头再望，看见那一家人还站在他离开他们的地方，大胡子正站在大路当中拥抱着他的孩子们。

===Original===
A bend in the path soon shut them from view, and then the Philosopher, fortified by food and the freshness of the morning, strode onwards singing for very joy. It was still early, but now the birds had eaten their breakfasts and were devoting themselves to each other. They rested side by side on the branches of the trees and on the hedges, they danced in the air in happy brotherhoods and they sang to one another amiable and pleasant ditties.

When the Philosopher had walked for a long time he felt a little weary and sat down to refresh himself in the shadow of a great tree. Hard by there was a house of rugged stone. Long years ago it had been a castle, and, even now, though patched by time and misfortune its front was warlike and frowning. While he sat a young woman came along the road and stood gazing earnestly at this house. Her hair was as black as night and as smooth as still water, but her face came so stormily forward that her quiet attitude had yet no quietness in it. To her, after a few moments, the Philosopher spoke.

“Girl,” said he, “why do you look so earnestly at the house?”

The girl turned her pale face and stared at him.

“I did not notice you sitting under the tree,” said she, and she came slowly forward.

“Sit down by me,” said the Philosopher, “and we will talk. If you are in any trouble tell it to me, and perhaps you will talk the heaviest part away.”

“I will sit beside you willingly,” said the girl, and she did so.

===Chinese===
路一转弯，很快便把他们遮住了。哲学家得了食物和清晨爽气的滋养，迈开大步往前走去，欢喜得放声歌唱。时候还早，可鸟儿们已经吃罢早饭，一心一意彼此相与起来。它们并肩歇在树枝上、树篱上，在空中结成欢快的兄弟之群翩翩起舞，彼此唱着和悦动听的小调。

哲学家走了很久，觉得有些乏了，便在一棵大树的荫下坐下来歇息。近旁有一座粗糙石头砌的房子。很多年前它是一座城堡，即便如今，虽经岁月与灾殃的缝补，它的正面仍旧好战而阴沉。他坐着的时候，一个年轻女子沿路走来，站住恳切地凝望着这座房子。她的头发黑得像夜，平滑得像止水，可她的脸那样风暴似地向前逼来，连她安静的姿势里也没有一丝安静。过了片刻，哲学家对她开了口。

「姑娘，」他说，「你为什么这样恳切地望着这座房子？」

姑娘转过来她苍白的脸，盯着他看。

「我没留意您坐在树下。」她说着，慢慢走上前来。

「坐到我身边来，」哲学家说，「咱们聊聊。你若有什么烦恼，说给我听，也许说着说着，最沉的那一块就化掉了。」

「我很愿意坐在您身边。」姑娘说着坐下了。

===Original===
“It is good to talk trouble over,” he continued. “Do you know that talk is a real thing? There is more power in speech than many people conceive. Thoughts come from God, they are born through the marriage of the head and the lungs. The head moulds the thought into the form of words, then it is borne and sounded on the air which has been already in the secret kingdoms of the body, which goes in bearing life and come out freighted with wisdom. For this reason a lie is very terrible, because it is turning mighty and incomprehensible things to base uses, and is burdening the life-giving element with a foul return for its goodness; but those who speak the truth and whose words are the symbols of wisdom and beauty, these purify the whole world and daunt contagion. The only trouble the body can know is disease. All other miseries come from the brain, and, as these belong to thought, they can be driven out by their master as unruly and unpleasant vagabonds; for a mental trouble should be spoken to, confronted, reprimanded and so dismissed. The brain cannot afford to harbour any but pleasant and eager citizens who will do their part in making laughter and holiness for the world, for that is the duty of thought.”

While the Philosopher spoke the girl had been regarding him steadfastly.

“Sir,” said she, “we tell our hearts to a young man and our heads to an old man, and when the heart is a fool the head is bound to be a liar. I can tell you the things I know, but how will I tell you the things I feel when I myself do not understand them? If I say these words to you ‘I love a man’ I do not say anything at all, and you do not hear one of the words which my heart is repeating over and over to itself in the silence of my body. Young people are fools in their heads and old people are fools in their hearts, and they can only look at each other and pass by in wonder.”

===Chinese===
「把烦恼谈出来是好事，」他接着说，「你可知道，谈话是一件实在的东西？言语里的力量，比许多人想的要大。思想来自上帝，它们诞生于头脑与肺腑的婚配。头脑把思想塑成字句的形状，随后它便承载着、鸣响在空气上——那空气早已到过身体内里的秘密王国，进去时载着生命，出来时满载智慧。因此谎言极其可怕，因为那是把强大而不可解的东西拿去作卑贱的用途，是以污秽回报那赋予生命的元素的善意；可那些讲真话的人，他们的话是智慧与美的象征，他们能净化整个世界，震慑疫疠。身体唯一能有的烦恼是疾病。其余一切苦楚都来自头脑；这些既属于思想，就能被它们的主人像驱逐不守法度的讨厌游民一样驱逐出去；因为心里的烦恼就该对它说话、与它对质、申斥它，然后打发它走。头脑供养不起别的房客，只供养快活而热忱的公民——他们要为世界制造欢笑与神圣尽力，因为那正是思想的职分。」

哲学家说话的时候，姑娘一直目不转睛地端详着他。

「先生，」她说，「我们把心事讲给年轻男人，把头脑的事讲给年老人；心里糊涂的时候，头脑必得撒谎。我能把我知道的事告诉您，可我连自己都不懂那些我感觉着的事，又怎么告诉您？倘若我对您说『我爱一个男人』，我等于什么也没说；我的心在我身体的静默里一遍又一遍念着的那句话，您一个字也没听见。年轻人头脑是傻子，老年人心里是傻子，他们只能彼此望一望，惊奇怪异地错身而过。」

===Original===
“You are wrong,” said the Philosopher. “An old person can take your hand like this and say, ‘May every good thing come to you, my daughter.’ For all trouble there is sympathy, and for love there is memory, and these are the head and the heart talking to each other in quiet friendship. What the heart knows today the head will understand tomorrow, and as the head must be the scholar of the heart it is necessary that our hearts be purified and free from every false thing, else we are tainted beyond personal redemption.”

“Sir,” said the girl, “I know of two great follies—they are love and speech, for when these are given they can never be taken back again, and the person to whom these are given is not any richer, but the giver is made poor and abashed. I gave my love to a man who did not want it. I told him of my love, and he lifted his eyelids at me; that is my trouble.”

For a moment the Philosopher sat in stricken silence looking on the ground. He had a strange disinclination to look at the girl although he felt her eyes fixed steadily on him. But in a little while he did look at her and spoke again.

“To carry gifts to an ungrateful person cannot be justified and need not be mourned for. If your love is noble why do you treat it meanly? If it is lewd the man was right to reject it.”

“We love as the wind blows,” she replied.

“There is a thing,” said the Philosopher, “and it is both the biggest and the littlest thing in the world.”

“What is that?” said the girl.

“It is pride,” he answered. “It lives in an empty house. The head which has never been visited by the heart is the house pride lives in. You are in error, my dear, and not in love. Drive out the knave pride, put a flower in your hair and walk freely again.”

The girl laughed, and suddenly her pale face became rosy as the dawn and as radiant and lovely as a cloud. She shed warmth and beauty about her as she leaned forward.

“You are wrong,” she whispered, “because he does love me; but he does not know it yet. He is young and full of fury, and has no time to look at women, but he looked at me. My heart knows it and my head knows it, but I am impatient and yearn for him to look at me again. His heart will remember me tomorrow, and he will come searching for me with prayers and tears, with shouts and threats. I will be very hard to find tomorrow when he holds out his arms to the air and the sky, and is astonished and frightened to find me nowhere. I will hide from him tomorrow, and frown at him when he speaks, and turn aside when he follows me: until the day after tomorrow when he will frighten me with his anger, and hold me with his furious hands, and make me look at him.”

===Chinese===
「你错了，」哲学家说，「上了年纪的人可以像这样拉住你的手，说：『愿一切好事都降临你，我的女儿。』一切烦恼都有同情来抚慰，一切爱都有记忆来存留；这便是头脑与心在安静的情谊里彼此交谈。心今天知道的，头脑明天就会明白；头脑既然要做心的学生，我们的心就必须纯净、不受一点虚假的玷染，否则我们便败坏得连自己也救赎不得。」

「先生，」姑娘说，「我知道有两件大傻事——那就是爱与言语：这两样东西一旦给了出去，就再也收不回来；领受的人不见得更富，给出的人却变得又穷又窘。我把我的爱给了一个不想要它的男人。我把爱告诉了他，他却只朝我抬了抬眼皮；这就是我的烦恼。」

有那么一刻，哲学家遭了击打似的默默坐着，望着地面。他奇怪地不愿意去看姑娘，尽管觉出她的眼睛定定地盯着自己。可过了一会儿，他还是看了看她，又开口了。

「把礼物送给不知感激的人，既无从辩解，也不必哀伤。你的爱若是高贵的，你为什么作践它？它若是下贱的，那男人拒绝它就拒绝对了。」

「我们爱，就像风吹。」她答道。

「有一样东西，」哲学家说，「它既是世上最大的，又是世上最小的。」

「是什么？」姑娘说。

「是骄傲，」他答道，「它住在一座空屋子里。从来不曾被心造访过的头脑，就是骄傲栖身的屋子。亲爱的，你是在错处，并不在爱里。把骄傲那个恶棍赶出去，往头发上插一朵花，重新大大方方地走路吧。」

姑娘笑了，她苍白的脸霎时红得像朝霞，亮得、美得像一朵云。她俯身向前，周身漾着暖意与美。

「你错了，」她低声说，「因为他确实爱我；只是他自己还不知道。他年轻，一腔火气，没工夫看女人，可他看了我。我的心知道，我的头也知道；可我不耐烦，巴望他再看我一眼。他的心明天会记起我，他会带着祷告和眼泪、叫喊和威吓来寻我。明天，等他朝空中、朝天上张开手臂，惊惶地发现哪里也找不到我，那时我偏要难找得很。明天我要躲开他，他一说话我就皱眉，他一跟着我我就转身走开——直到后天，他会用他的怒气吓我，用他狂暴的双手抓住我，逼我看他。」

===Original===
Saying this the girl arose and prepared to go away.

“He is in that house,” said she, “and I would not let him see me here for anything in the world.”

“You have wasted all my time,” said the Philosopher, smiling.

“What else is time for?” said the girl, and she kissed the Philosopher and ran swiftly down the road.

She had been gone but a few moments when a man came out of the grey house and walked quickly across the grass. When he reached the hedge separating the field from the road he tossed his two arms in the air, swung them down, and jumped over the hedge into the roadway. He was a short, dark youth, and so swift and sudden were his movements that he seemed to look on every side at the one moment although he bore furiously to his own direction.

The Philosopher addressed him mildly.

“That was a good jump,” said he.

The young man spun around from where he stood, and was by the Philosopher’s side in an instant.

“It would be a good jump for other men,” said he, “but it is only a little jump for me. You are very dusty, sir; you must have travelled a long distance today.”

“A long distance,” replied the Philosopher. “Sit down here, my friend, and keep me company for a little time.”

“I do not like sitting down,” said the young man, “but I always consent to a request, and I always accept friendship.” And, so saying, he threw himself down on the grass.

===Chinese===
说完这话，姑娘站起身，准备离去。

「他就在那座房子里，」她说，「说什么也不能让他看见我在这儿。」

「你把我全部的时间都耽误了。」哲学家微笑着说。

「时间不干这个还干什么？」姑娘说着，吻了哲学家，顺着路飞快地跑走了。

她走了没几分钟，一个男子从那座灰房子里出来，快步穿过草地。走到隔开田野与大路的树篱前，他把双臂往空中一扬，往下一摆，跃过树篱跳到路上。他是个矮小结实的黑发青年，动作又快又突然，仿佛同一瞬里眼睛顾到了四面八方，脚下却又狂暴地只朝自己的方向奔去。

哲学家温和地同他搭话。

「跳得好。」他说。

青年人在原地一个旋转，转眼已到了哲学家身边。

「这对别人来说是跳得好，」他说，「对我只是小小一跳。您满身尘土，先生；您今天一定走了很远的路。」

「很远，」哲学家答道，「在这儿坐下吧，朋友，陪我待一会儿。」

「我不喜欢坐下，」青年人说，「但我从不拒绝请求，也从不拒绝友谊。」说着，他一屁股躺在草地上。

===Original===
“Do you work in that big house?” said the Philosopher.

“I do,” he replied. “I train the hounds for a fat, jovial man, full of laughter and insolence.”

“I think you do not like your master.”

“Believe, sir, that I do not like any master; but this man I hate. I have been a week in his service, and he has not once looked on me as on a friend. This very day, in the kennel, he passed me as though I were a tree or a stone. I almost leaped to catch him by the throat and say: ‘Dog, do you not salute your fellow man?’ But I looked after him and let him go, for it would be an unpleasant thing to strangle a fat person.”

“If you are displeased with your master should you not look for another occupation?” said the Philosopher.

“I was thinking of that, and I was thinking whether I ought to kill him or marry his daughter. She would have passed me by as her father did, but I would not let a woman do that to me: no man would.”

“What did you do to her?” said the Philosopher.

The young man chuckled—

“I did not look at her the first time, and when she came near me the second time I looked another way, and on the third day she spoke to me, and while she stood I looked over her shoulder distantly. She said she hoped I would be happy in my new home, and she made her voice sound pleasant while she said it; but I thanked her and turned away carelessly.”

“Is the girl beautiful?” said the Philosopher.

“I do not know,” he replied; “I have not looked at her yet, although now I see her everywhere. I think she is a woman who would annoy me if I married her.”

“If you haven’t seen her, how can you think that?”

“She has tame feet,” said the youth. “I looked at them and they got frightened. Where have you travelled from, sir?”

“I will tell you that,” said the Philosopher, “if you will tell me your name.”

“It is easily told,” he answered; “my name is MacCulain.”

“When I came last night,” said the Philosopher, “from the place of Angus Óg in the cave of the Sleepers of Erinn I was bidden say to a man named MacCulain that The Grey of Macha had neighed in his sleep and the sword of Laeg clashed on the floor as he turned in his slumber.”

The young man leaped from the grass.

“Sir,” said he in a strained voice, “I do not understand your words, but they make my heart to dance and sing within me like a bird.”

“If you listen to your heart,” said the Philosopher, “you will learn every good thing, for the heart is the fountain of wisdom tossing its thoughts up to the brain which gives them form,”—and, so saying, he saluted the youth and went again on his way by the curving road.

===Chinese===
「你在那座大房子里做事？」哲学家问。

「是，」他答道，「我替一个肥胖快活的男人驯猎犬，那人满肚子笑，也满肚子无礼。」

「我看你不喜欢你的主人。」

「请相信，先生，我不喜欢任何主人；对这一个，我是恨。我到他手下做了一个礼拜，他没有一次把我当朋友看上一眼。就在今天，在犬舍里，他从我身边走过，就当我是棵树、是块石头。我差点跳起来掐住他的喉咙，对他说：『畜生，你不同你的同类打招呼吗？』可我只是望着他走远，放他去了——掐死一个胖子，终究是件不愉快的事。」

「既然不喜欢主人，何不另找一份差事？」哲学家说。

「我正想着呢。我也在想要不要杀了他，还是娶了他的女儿。她本想学她父亲那样从我身边走过，可我不能让女人这样待我：谁都不能。」

「你对她做了什么？」哲学家问。

青年人嘿嘿一笑——

「头一回，我没有看她；第二回她走近我，我把眼光放到别处；第三天，她先开了口，她站着说话，我却越过她的肩膀望向远处。她说她祝我在新家快活，说这话时还把嗓音放得好听；我谢了她，漫不经心地转身走了。」

「那姑娘美吗？」哲学家问。

「不知道，」他答道，「我还没有看过她，虽然现在我到处都看见她。我想，她是个要是娶了她就会惹我恼火的女人。」

「你既然没见过她，怎么会这么想？」

「她有一双怯生生的脚，」青年人说，「我看了它们一眼，它们就害怕了。——您从哪里来的，先生？」

「我乐意奉告，」哲学家说，「只要你肯告诉我你的名字。」

「这好说，」他答道，「我叫麦克库兰（MacCulain）。」

「昨夜，」哲学家说，「我从爱林沉睡者之洞安格斯·奥格的居所回来时，受托向一位名叫麦克库兰的人传话：马查的灰马（the Grey of Macha）在睡梦里嘶鸣，拉埃格（Laeg）的剑在他翻身时当啷落地。」

青年人从草地上跳了起来。

「先生，」他声音发紧地说，「我不懂您的话，可它们使我心里的一只鸟又叫又跳。」

「你若肯听你的心，」哲学家说，「你就会学到一切好的东西，因为心是智慧的泉，把它的思想抛给头脑，头脑再给它们形状。」说着，他向青年人致意，顺着弯曲的路又上路了。

===Original===
Now the day had advanced, noon was long past, and the strong sunlight blazed ceaselessly on the world. His path was still on the high mountains, running on for a short distance and twisting perpetually to the right hand and to the left. One might scarcely call it a path, it grew so narrow. Sometimes, indeed, it almost ceased to be a path, for the grass had stolen forward inch by inch to cover up the tracks of man. There were no hedges but rough, tumbled ground only, which was patched by trailing bushes and stretched away in mounds and hummocks beyond the far horizon. There was a deep silence everywhere, not painful, for where the sun shines there is no sorrow: the only sound to be heard was the swish of long grasses against his feet as he trod, and the buzz of an occasional bee that came and was gone in an instant.

The Philosopher was very hungry, and he looked about on all sides to see if there was anything he might eat. “If I were a goat or a cow,” said he, “I could eat this grass and be nourished. If I were a donkey I could crop the hard thistles which are growing on every hand, or if I were a bird I could feed on the caterpillars and creeping things which stir innumerably everywhere. But a man may not eat even in the midst of plenty, because he has departed from nature, and lives by crafty and twisted thought.”

Speaking in this manner he chanced to lift his eyes from the ground and saw, far away, a solitary figure which melted into the folding earth and reappeared again in a different place. So peculiar and erratic were the movements of this figure that the Philosopher had great difficulty in following it, and, indeed, would have been unable to follow, but that the other chanced in his direction. When they came nearer he saw it was a young boy, who was dancing hither and thither in any and every direction. A bushy mound hid him for an instant, and the next they were standing face to face staring at each other. After a moment’s silence the boy, who was about twelve years of age, and as beautiful as the morning, saluted the Philosopher.

“Have you lost your way, sir?” said he.

“All paths,” the Philosopher replied, “are on the earth, and so one can never be lost—but I have lost my dinner.”

The boy commenced to laugh.

“What are you laughing at, my son?” said the Philosopher.

“Because,” he replied, “I am bringing you your dinner. I wondered what sent me out in this direction, for I generally go more to the east.”

“Have you got my dinner?” said the Philosopher anxiously.

“I have,” said the boy: “I ate my own dinner at home, and I put your dinner in my pocket. I thought,” he explained, “that I might be hungry if I went far away.”

“The gods directed you,” said the Philosopher.

“They often do,” said the boy, and he pulled a small parcel from his pocket.

The Philosopher instantly sat down, and the boy handed him the parcel. He opened this and found bread and cheese.

“It’s a good dinner,” said he, and commenced to eat.

“Would you not like a piece also, my son?”

“I would like a little piece,” said the boy, and he sat down before the Philosopher, and they ate together happily.

===Chinese===
这时天已大进，正午早过，强烈的阳光不停地炙照着世界。他的路仍在高山上，短短地伸出去一段，又不停向左向右拧转。那简直算不上是路，它窄得厉害。有时它实在几乎不成其为路了，因为青草一寸一寸地悄悄漫上来，要盖住人的足迹。没有树篱，只有高低翻腾的荒地，匍匐的灌木一簇簇补缀其间，土墩土丘连绵起伏，一直铺到远远的天边。到处是一片深深的寂静，却并不苦楚，因为有太阳的地方就没有哀愁：唯一听得见的，是他行走时长草扫过脚面的沙沙声，和偶尔一只蜜蜂嗡的一声来了又没了。

哲学家饿得很了，四下张望有什么可吃的。「我要是头山羊或者母牛，」他说，「就能吃这草，吃了还长膘。我要是头驴，就能啃遍地都是的硬蓟草；我要是只鸟，就能啄食到处蠢动的无数毛虫和爬虫。可是人守着满地的充裕也不能吃，因为他离开了自然，靠狡诈扭曲的思想过活。」

正这样说着，他碰巧从地上抬起眼来，远远望见一个孤零零的身影，忽而隐入起伏的地面，忽而又在另一处冒出来。那身影的行动那样古怪、那样没个定准，哲学家费了好大劲才盯住它；实在说，若不是那一位恰好朝他这个方向来，他根本跟不住。走近了他才看清，那是个年少的孩子，正朝着四面八方随处乱跳地舞蹈。一座长满灌木的土墩把他遮了一瞬，下一瞬，两人已面对面站着，彼此对望。静了片刻，那孩子——他约莫十二岁，美得像清晨一样——向哲学家行了礼。

「您迷路了吗，先生？」他说。

「天下的路，」哲学家答道，「都在地上，所以人永远不会迷路——只是我把午饭丢了。」

孩子笑了起来。

「你笑什么，我的孩子？」哲学家说。

「因为，」他答道，「我正是给您送午饭来的。我还纳闷今天是什么把我引到这个方向来了，我平常更爱往东边走。」

「你带着我的午饭？」哲学家急切地说。

「带着呢，」孩子说，「我在家吃了自己的午饭，把您的午饭装进了口袋。我想，」他解释道，「要是走远了，说不定会饿。」

「是神指引了你。」哲学家说。

「他们常常这样。」孩子说着，从口袋里掏出一个小包。

哲学家当场坐下，孩子把小包递给他。他打开一看，是面包和奶酪。

「这是顿好饭。」他说着吃了起来。

「你也来一块吧，我的孩子？」

「我想要一小块。」孩子说。他在哲学家面前坐下，两人快快活活地一起吃了。

===Original===
When they had finished the Philosopher praised the gods, and then said, more to himself than to the boy:

“If I had a little drink of water I would want nothing else.”

“There is a stream four paces from here,” said his companion. “I will get some water in my cap,” and he leaped away.

In a few moments he came back holding his cap tenderly, and the Philosopher took this and drank the water.

“I want nothing more in the world,” said he, “except to talk with you. The sun is shining, the wind is pleasant, and the grass is soft. Sit down beside me again for a little time.”

So the boy sat down, and the Philosopher lit his pipe.

“Do you live far from here?” said he.

“Not far,” said the boy. “You could see my mother’s house from this place if you were as tall as a tree, and even from the ground you can see a shape of smoke yonder that floats over our cottage.”

The Philosopher looked but could see nothing.

“My eyes are not as good as yours are,” said he, “because I am getting old.”

“What does it feel like to be old?” said the boy.

“It feels stiff like,” said the Philosopher.

“Is that all?” said the boy.

“I don’t know,” the Philosopher replied after a few moments’ silence. “Can you tell me what it looks like to be young?”

“Why not?” said the boy, and then a slight look of perplexity crossed his face, and he continued, “I don’t think I can.”

“Young people,” said the Philosopher, “do not know what age is, and old people forget what youth was. When you begin to grow old always think deeply of your youth, for an old man without memories is a wasted life, and nothing is worth remembering but our childhood. I will tell you some of the differences between being old and young, and then you can ask me questions, and so we will get at both sides of the matter. First, an old man gets tired quicker than a boy.”

The boy thought for a moment, and then replied:

“That is not a great difference, for a boy does get very tired.”

The Philosopher continued:

“An old man does not want to eat as often as a boy.”

“That is not a great difference either,” the boy replied, “for they both do eat. Tell me the big difference.”

“I do not know it, my son; but I have always thought there was a big difference. Perhaps it is that an old man has memories of things which a boy cannot even guess at.”

“But they both have memories,” said the boy, laughing, “and so it is not a big difference.”

“That is true,” said the Philosopher. “Maybe there is not so much difference after all. Tell me things you do, and we will see if I can do them also.”

“But I don’t know what I do,” he replied.

“You must know the things you do,” said the Philosopher, “but you may not understand how to put them in order. The great trouble about any kind of examination is to know where to begin, but there are always two places in everything with which we can commence—they are the beginning and the end. From either of these points a view may be had which comprehends the entire period. So we will begin with the things you did this morning.”

“I am satisfied with that,” said the boy.

===Chinese===
吃完，哲学家赞美了众神，随后与其说对孩子、不如说对自己说道：

「要是再有一小口水喝，我就别无所求了。」

「离这儿四步有一条溪，」同伴说，「我用帽子给你舀水来。」他一跃而去。

不多时他捧着帽子小心翼翼地回来了，哲学家接过来，喝了那水。

「这世上我再无所求了，」他说，「只想同你说说话。太阳照着，风很和畅，草也柔软。再坐到我身边来一会儿吧。」

于是孩子坐下，哲学家点起烟斗。

「你住得远吗？」他说。

「不远，」孩子说，「您要是有一棵树那么高，从这儿就望得见我母亲的房子；就算站在地上，您也看得见那边有一缕烟的影子，飘在我们小屋的上头。」

哲学家望了望，可什么也看不见。

「我的眼睛不如你的好，」他说，「因为我老了。」

「老了是什么滋味？」孩子问。

「觉得僵了。」哲学家说。

「就这些？」孩子说。

「不知道，」哲学家默了片刻答道，「你能告诉我，年轻看上去是什么样子吗？」

「怎么不能。」孩子说。随后一丝困惑掠过他的脸，他接着说：「我想我说不上来。」

「年轻人，」哲学家说，「不知道老是什么；老年人，忘了年轻是什么。等你开始变老，要常常深念你的青春，因为没有记忆的老年是一场虚掷的人生，而值得记起的，只有我们的童年。我来讲讲老和年轻的几样分别，然后你可以问我问题，咱们把这桩事的两面都弄清楚。第一，老年人比孩子疲得快。」

孩子想了一会儿，答道：

「这不算大分别，孩子也确实很累的。」

哲学家接着说：

「老年人不像孩子那样常常想吃。」

「这也不算大分别，」孩子答道，「因为两个都吃。告诉我大的分别。」

「我不知道，我的孩子；可我一向觉得有个大分别。也许，是老年人记得许多孩子连猜也猜不着的事。」

「可两个都有记忆，」孩子笑着说，「所以不算大分别。」

「这话对，」哲学家说，「也许终究没有那么多分别。告诉我你做的事，咱们看看我还能不能做。」

「可我不知道我做什么。」他答道。

「你做的事你一定知道，」哲学家说，「只是未必知道怎么把它们排个次序。凡属考核，大麻烦就在于不知从哪儿开头；可万事总有两处可以着手——就是开头和结尾。从这两点上随便哪一点，都能望见全程。那咱们就从你今天早晨做的事开头。」

「这样我很满意。」孩子说。

===Original===
The Philosopher then continued:

“When you awakened this morning and went out of the house what was the first thing you did?”

The boy thought—

“I went out, then I picked up a stone and threw it into the field as far as I could.”

“What then?” said the Philosopher.

“Then I ran after the stone to see could I catch up on it before it hit the ground.”

“Yes,” said the Philosopher.

“I ran so fast that I tumbled over myself into the grass.”

“What did you do after that?”

“I lay where I fell and plucked handfuls of the grass with both hands and threw them on my back.”

“Did you get up then?”

“No, I pressed my face into the grass and shouted a lot of times with my mouth against the ground, and then I sat up and did not move for a long time.”

“Were you thinking?” said the Philosopher.

“No, I was not thinking or doing anything.”

“Why did you do all these things?” said the Philosopher.

“For no reason at all,” said the boy.

“That,” said the Philosopher triumphantly, “is the difference between age and youth. Boys do things for no reason, and old people do not. I wonder do we get old because we do things by reason instead of instinct?”

“I don’t know,” said the boy, “everything gets old. Have you travelled very far today, sir?”

“I will tell you that if you will tell me your name.”

“My name,” said the boy, “is MacCushin.”

“When I came last night,” said the Philosopher, “from the place of Angus Óg in the Cave of the Sleepers I was bidden say to one named MacCushin that a son would be born to Angus Óg and his wife, Caitilin, and that the sleepers of Erinn had turned in their slumbers.”

The boy regarded him steadfastly.

“I know,” said he, “why Angus Óg sent me that message. He wants me to make a poem to the people of Erinn, so that when the Sleepers arise they will meet with friends.”

“The Sleepers have arisen,” said the Philosopher. “They are about us on every side. They are walking now, but they have forgotten their names and the meanings of their names. You are to tell them their names and their lineage, for I am an old man, and my work is done.”

“I will make a poem some day,” said the boy, “and every man will shout when he hears it.”

“God be with you, my son,” said the Philosopher, and he embraced the boy and went forward on his journey.

===Chinese===
哲学家接着说：

「今天早晨你醒来出了门，头一件事做的是什么？」

孩子想了想——

「我出了门，然后捡起一块石头，使劲往田里扔，扔得越远越好。」

「然后呢？」哲学家说。

「然后我就追那块石头，看能不能赶在它落地前追上它。」

「唔。」哲学家说。

「我跑得太快，一个跟头栽进草里。」

「那之后你做了什么？」

「我就躺在摔倒的地方，双手拔了一把一把的草，扔在自己背上。」

「然后你起来了吗？」

「没有，我把脸埋进草里，嘴贴着地喊了好一阵，然后坐起来，很久很久没动。」

「你在想事情？」哲学家说。

「没有，我什么也没想，什么也没做。」

「你做这些事是为什么？」哲学家说。

「什么也不为。」孩子说。

「这就对啦，」哲学家得意地说，「这就是老年和年轻的分别。孩子做事什么也不为，老年人做事总要有个为什么。我疑心，我们是不是就因为凡事都要个为什么、不凭本能，才变老的？」

「不知道，」孩子说，「样样都会老的。——您今天走了很远吗，先生？」

「你告诉我你的名字，我就告诉你。」

「我的名字，」孩子说，「叫麦克库辛（MacCushin）。」

「昨夜，」哲学家说，「我从沉睡者之洞安格斯·奥格的居所回来时，受托向一位名叫麦克库辛的人传话：安格斯·奥格和他的妻子卡蒂琳将得一个儿子，爱林的沉睡者们已在睡梦里翻了身。」

孩子定定地望着他。

「我知道，」他说，「安格斯·奥格为什么给我送这个口信。他要我给爱林的人民作一首诗，好教沉睡者们起身时，迎接他们的是朋友。」

「沉睡者们已经起身了，」哲学家说，「他们就在我们四下里。他们如今正走着路，只是忘了自己的名字和名字的含义。你要把他们的名字和他们的世系告诉他们，因为我已经老了，我的事做完了。」

「有一天我要作一首诗，」孩子说，「人人听见了都要欢呼。」

「上帝与你同在，我的孩子。」哲学家说着，拥抱了孩子，继续赶路。

===Original===
About half an hour’s easy travelling brought him to a point from which he could see far down below to the pine trees of Coille Doraca. The shadowy evening had crept over the world ere he reached the wood, and when he entered the little house the darkness had already descended.
